On the Composition of Drugs by Kind

De compositione medicamentorum per genera · Kühn XIII 362-1058

The first complete English translation we know of. No complete translation in any modern language. Parts exist in English: G. Baker 1574 (book 3, from a French epitome), M. Marai 2023 (substantial excerpts, Warwick PhD thesis) and ten reference passages (galen_project, 2026). This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. Greek (Kühn)

τηνικαῦτα γὰρ ἑτέρων τε πολλῶν ἀπώλοντο βιβλία καὶ τῶν ἐμῶν ὅσα κατὰ τὴν ἀποθήκην ἐκείνην ἔκειτο, μηδενὸς τῶν ἐν Ῥώμῃ 363 φίλων ἔχειν ὁμολογοῦντος ἀντίγραφα τῶν πρώτων δυοῖν. ἐγκειμένων οὖν τῶν ἑταίρων αὖθίς με γράψαι τὴν αὐτὴν πραγματείαν, ἀναγκαῖον ἔδοξέ μοι δηλῶσαι περὶ τῶν προεκδοθέντων, ὅπως μή τις προεντυχὼν αὐτοῖς ποτε ζητοίη τὴν αἰτίαν τοῦ δίς με περὶ τῶν αὐτῶν πραγματεύσασθαι. κατ’ ἐκεῖνα μὲν οὖν ἐποιησάμην τὸν λόγον ἀπὸ τῆς ἀρχῆς τοῦ πρώτου βιβλίου πρὸς τοὺς σοφιστικῶς ἀναιροῦντας, ὡς οἷόν τε τὴν ἐπαγγελίαν τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων, ἐνίοις δὲ καὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν ἐπηρεάζοντας, ἅμα τοῦ σκώπτειν τοὺς γεγραφότας ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν, ὡς τὰ μὲν ἧπαρ ὠφελεῖ, τὰ δὲ σπλῆνα, τὰ δὲ νεφροὺς ἢ κύστιν ἢ κεφαλὴν ἢ πνεύμονα. πότερα γὰρ, φασὶν, ἐφεστηκώς τις αὐτοῖς προστάττει, καθάπερ ἐν τῇ τραγῳδίᾳ, Σὺ μὲν βάδιζε τὰς ἐπ’ Ἰνάχου ῥοὰς, Σὺ δ’ ἀμφὶ Θήβας στεῖχε τὰς Καδμηΐας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For at that time the books of many others perished, and of mine all those that were lying in that storehouse, since none of my friends in 363 Rome acknowledged having copies of the first two. Since, then, my companions pressed me to write the same treatise again, it seemed necessary to me to make a declaration about the previously published ones, so that no one who had earlier come across them might ever seek the reason why I have treated the same matters twice. In those, then, I made my argument from the beginning of the first book against those who sophistically do away, as far as they can, with the professed virtues of compound drugs, and who also abuse some of the simple ones, at the same time mocking those who have written about them, on the ground that some benefit the liver, others the spleen, others the kidneys or bladder or head or lung. 'For does someone,' they say, 'standing over them give them orders, as in the tragedy: "You, go to the streams of Inachus; and you, proceed around Cadmean Thebes"?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπαγγελίαν, ὡς οἷόν τε, ἐπηρεάζοντας, ὁμολογοῦντος

For at that time the books of many others perished, and also those of mine that were lying in that storehouse, and none of my friends in Rome 363 acknowledged having copies of the first two. Since, then, my companions were pressing me to write the same treatise again, it seemed necessary to me to make clear the facts about the books previously published, so that no one who had come across them earlier might ever seek the reason why I have treated the same subjects twice. In those books, then, from the beginning of the first book, I directed my argument against those who sophistically do away, so far as they can, with the claim of compound drugs, and who also abusively attack some of the simple ones as well, at the same time mocking those who have written about them, to the effect that some benefit the liver, others the spleen, others the kidneys or bladder or head or lung. For, they say, does someone stand over them and give them orders, as in the tragedy: 'You, go to the streams of Inachus; and you, march about Cadmean Thebes'?

Greek (Kühn)

οὕτως ἐνταῦθα σὺ μὲν βάδιζε τὰς ἐφ’ ἥπατι πύλας, σὺ δ’ ἐπί τε κύστιν ἢ καὶ νεφροὺς δύο ἢ τὸ καταποθὲν εἰς τὴν κοιλίαν ἀναδίδοται μὲν εἰς ἧπαρ, ἐντεῦθεν δὲ φέρεται πάντῃ 364 τοῦ σώματος; ἀλλὰ τοῦτό γε τὸ σκῶμμα δηλοῖ τοὺς ταῦτα λέγοντας εἰς τοσοῦτον ἀπείρους εἶναι φαρμάκων δυνάμεως, ὡς ἀγνοεῖν ὑπὸ μὲν τοῦ θαλαττίου λαγωοῦ μόνον τῶν πάντων ἐν τῷ σώματι μορίων ἑλκόμενον πνεύμονα, κύστιν δ’ αὖ μόνην ὑπὸ τῶν κανθαρίδων. ὑπ’ ἄλλων δὲ τοὺς ἐν νεφροῖς λίθους θρυπτομένους, ὑφ’ ἑτέρων δὲ τὰς ἐκ πνεύμονός τε καὶ θώρακος ἀναπτύσεις εὐπετεῖς γινομένας, ὥσπερ ὑπ’ ἄλλων ἄλλα καθ’ ἕν τι μόριον ἐξαιρέτως ἐπιτελούμενα. πολυειδῶς δὲ τοῦτον τὸν λόγον ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶν γεγραμμένων ἤδη μοι πρόσθεν, ὡς ἔφην, ὑπομνημάτων ἐξειργασάμην, ὥσπερ αὖ κατὰ δεύτερον ἄλλον, οἰομένων τινῶν ἐν τῇ συνθέσει διαφθείρεσθαι παντάπασι τὰς ἐναντίας ἀλλήλαις δυνάμεις τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων. εἰ γὰρ ὕδωρ, φασὶ, τὸ ζεστότατον τῷ ψυχροτάτῳ μίξεις, οὐδέτερον μενεῖ τῶν μιχθέντων ὁποῖον ἦν ἔμπροσθεν, ἀλλ’ ἕν τι γενήσεται τρίτον, ἕτερον ἀμφοῖν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

So here: 'you go to the gates at the liver, and you to the bladder or indeed the two kidneys' — or is what has been swallowed into the belly distributed to the liver and from there carried to every part 364 of the body? But this jest at any rate shows that those who say these things are so inexperienced in the power of drugs as not to know that by the sea-hare the lung alone of all the parts in the body is ulcerated, and the bladder alone, in turn, by cantharides, and that by other drugs the stones in the kidneys are broken up, and by yet others the expectorations from the lung and the thorax are made easy, just as by others still other effects are brought about distinctively in some one part. I worked out this argument in many forms in the first of the treatises already written by me before, as I said, just as again in the second I worked out another, since some think that in composition the powers of the simple drugs that are contrary to one another are entirely destroyed. For if, they say, you mix the hottest water with the coldest, neither of the things mixed will remain such as it was before, but some one third thing will come to be, different from both.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἑλκόμενον (taken as for ἑλκούμενον, 'ulcerated'; as printed it would mean 'drawn'), ἐπί τε κύστιν (τε awkward; text possibly corrupt), κατὰ δεύτερον ἄλλον (construction elliptical; 'another [argument] in the second [book]')

so here: 'You, go to the gates at the liver; you, to the bladder, or else to the two kidneys'? Or is what has been swallowed into the stomach distributed to the liver, and from there carried to every part 364 of the body? But this very jibe shows that those who say these things are so inexperienced in the power of drugs as not to know that the lung alone, of all the parts in the body, is ulcerated by the sea hare, and again the bladder alone by cantharides; that by others the stones in the kidneys are broken up, and by others expectorations from the lung and chest are made easy, just as by others other effects are brought about specifically in some one part. This argument I worked out in many forms in the first of the books I had written earlier, as I said, just as in the second I worked out another, since some think that in compounding the mutually opposed powers of the simple drugs are entirely destroyed. For if, they say, you mix the most boiling-hot water with the coldest, neither of the things mixed will remain as it was before, but some one third thing will come into being, different from both.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐδείχθησαν δὲ κᾀνταῦθα ληροῦντες μάταια διὰ τὸ μὴ γινώσκειν ἔνια μὲν τῶν προσφερομένων τῷ σώματι κατὰ τὰς ἐμφύτους δυνάμεις, ἔνια δὲ κατὰ τὰς ἐπικτήτους 365 ποιότητας ἐνεργεῖν. τίς γὰρ οὐκ οἶδε κώνειον ἢ μηκώνειον ψύχοντα; καὶ μὴν εἰ θερμήνας αὐτὰ σφοδρῶς, προσφέροις ζώου σώματι, καυτηρίῳ παραπλησίως ἕλκος ἐργάσῃ. καθάπερ γε πάλιν εἰ νᾶπυ τρίψας ἐν ἀέρι ψυχρῷ, μεθ’ ὕδατος ψυχροτάτου χειμῶνος ὥρᾳ, προσενέγκῃς ἐξαίφνης ἀραιῷ τε καὶ ἀσθενεῖ σώματι, φρίξει οὐδὲν ἧττον ἢ εἰ χιὼν ἢ κρύσταλλος ἔψαυσεν αὐτοῦ. ἀλλ’ ἐὰν ὁμιλήσῃ σώματι ζώου χρόνῳ πλέονι, πρῶτα μὲν αἴσθησιν ἐργάσεται θερμασίας ὀλίγης, εἶτα πλείονος, εἶθ’ οὕτω πολλῆς, ὡς ὑπὸ πυρὸς καίεσθαι δοκεῖν. ἔμπαλιν δὲ κώνειόν τε καὶ μηκώνειον θερμὰ προσαχθέντα, ἐὰν ὁμιλήσῃ τῷ σώματι χρόνῳ πλέονι, τοσαύτην ἐργάσεται τὴν ψύξιν, ὡς ἀναίσθητα γενέσθαι τὰ σώματα, κᾂν πρότερον αἰσθητικώτατα ὑπάρχῃ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But here too they were shown to be talking idle nonsense, because they did not know that some of the things applied to the body act according to their innate powers, others according to their acquired 365 qualities. For who does not know that hemlock or poppy-juice are cooling? And yet, if you heat them strongly and apply them to the body of an animal, you will produce an ulcer much as a cautery does. Just as, again, if you grind mustard in cold air, with very cold water, in the season of winter, and apply it suddenly to a loose-textured and weak body, it will shiver no less than if snow or ice had touched it. But if it remains in contact with the body of an animal for a longer time, it will first produce a sensation of slight heat, then of more, then of so much that one seems to be burned by fire. Conversely, hemlock and poppy-juice applied hot, if they remain in contact with the body for a longer time, will produce so great a cooling that the bodies become insensible, even if they were previously most sensitive.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀραιῷ, φρίξει

But here too they were shown to be talking idle nonsense, because they do not know that some of the things applied to the body act according to their innate powers, and some according to their acquired 365 qualities. For who does not know that hemlock or poppy juice cools? And yet, if you heat them strongly and apply them to the body of an animal, you will produce an ulcer much as a cautery does. So too, conversely, if you grind mustard in cold air, with very cold water in the winter season, and apply it suddenly to a rare and weak body, the body will shiver no less than if snow or ice had touched it. But if it stays in contact with the body of an animal for a longer time, it will first produce a sensation of slight heat, then of more, and then of so much that the body seems to be burned by fire. Conversely, hemlock and poppy juice applied hot, if they stay in contact with the body for a longer time, will produce so great a cooling that the bodies become insensible, even if they were previously most sensitive.

K13.365-366K. XIII 365–366, ch. 1.1 difficulty low (1.57/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

διωρισμένων οὖν ἡμῖν τῶν τοιούτων ἁπάντων ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως ὑπομνήμασιν, ἕνδεκα τὸν ἀριθμὸν οὖσιν, ἐπ’ αὐτὴν ἤδη τρέψομαι τὴν χρήσιμον διδασκαλίαν, ἐν ᾗ γυμνασάμενός τις, ἐὰν καὶ μηδ’ ὅλως ἐπιφέρηται τὰς γραφὰς τῶν πολυφαρμάκων δυνάμεων, οὐκ ἀπορήσει τῶν ἐπιτηδείων πρὸς ἕκαστον 366 πάθος, ἐὰν οὗτος, ὡς ἔφην, ἔμπειρος ᾖ τῆς ὕλης τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων. ἐν παρέργῳ δὲ ὁ λόγος οὗτος ἐπιδείκνυσι καὶ τὴν τῶν ἐμπειρικῶν ἰατρῶν φιλονεικίαν εἰς ὀνείρατά τε καὶ τύχην καὶ περίπτωσιν ἀναγόντων τὰ σύνθετα φάρμακα σχεδὸν ἅπαντα, πλὴν ὀλιγίστων τινῶν, ἃ κατὰ τὸν κοινὸν ἁπάντων ἀνθρώπων λόγον, ὃν ἐπιλογισμὸν αὐτοὶ καλοῦσιν, εὑρηκέναι φασί. ὥσπερ ὅταν ἀλλήλοις μίξωμιν φάρμακα τὴν αὐτὴν ἐπιδεδειγμένα διὰ τῆς πείρας ἐνέργειαν, ἄλλο δ’ ἐπ’ ἄλλου σώματος ἄμεινον ἢ χεῖρον ἐνηργηκὸς, εὔλογον μὲν ἡμῖν ἔδοξε, φασὶν ἔνιοι τῶν ἐμπειρικῶν, οὐ γὰρ δὴ πάντες γε, συνθεῖναι πλείω τοιαῦτα χάριν τοῦ κᾂν ἓν ἐξ αὐτῶν εὑρεθῆναι τῇ φύσει τοῦ θεραπευομένου σώματος οἰκεῖον. ὅπερ ἐπὶ τούτου δὴ τοῦ καινοτάτου φαρμάκου τοῦ καλουμένου κεφαλικοῦ γεγονέναι φασὶ, μιχθέντων ἐν αὐτῷ πάντων τῶν καὶ κατὰ μόνας ἐγνωσμένων ἕλκη σαρκοῦν. ἀλλὰ κατὰ τίνα γε λόγον ἰὸς μιχθεὶς ἁπλῇ κηρωτῇ σαρκωτικὸν ἐργάζεται φάρμακον, οὐδετέρου τῶν μιχθέντων σαρκοῦντος, οὐκέτι λέγουσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μίξωμιν

Since, then, all such matters have been defined by us in the commentaries On the Power of Simple Drugs, which are eleven in number, I shall now turn to the useful teaching itself, in which, once someone has exercised himself, even if he carries about with him no written formulae at all of the many-ingredient preparations, he will not be at a loss for the remedies suitable for each 366 affection, provided that he, as I said, is experienced in the material of simple drugs. Incidentally, this discourse also demonstrates the contentiousness of the Empiricist physicians, who refer nearly all compound drugs to dreams and chance and accidental encounter, except for some very few, which they say they have discovered according to the reasoning common to all human beings, which they themselves call epilogismos. As when we mix with one another (μίξωμιν, read μίξωμεν) drugs that have displayed through experience the same action, but one having acted better or worse on one body and another on another: "it seemed reasonable to us," say some of the Empiricists—for certainly not all of them—"to compound several such drugs together, so that at least one of them might be found to be appropriate to the nature of the body being treated." This, they say, is what happened in the case of this very novel drug called the "cephalic", all those ingredients having been mixed in it which are known, even singly, to produce flesh in wounds. But by what rationale verdigris, when mixed with a simple cerate, produces a flesh-producing drug, though neither of the ingredients mixed produces flesh, they no longer say.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πολυφαρμάκων δυνάμεων, καινοτάτου, ἐπιφέρηται

Since, then, all such matters have been determined by us in the commentaries On the Power of Simple Drugs, which are eleven in number, I shall now turn to the useful instruction itself. Whoever has trained himself in it, even if he does not carry with him at all the written formulas of the many-ingredient preparations, will not be at a loss for the remedies suitable to each 366 affection, provided that he, as I said, is experienced in the material of simple drugs. As a by-product, this account also exposes the contentiousness of the Empirical physicians, who trace back nearly all compound drugs, except for a very few, to dreams, chance and accidental encounter (periptōsis). These few they say they discovered by the reasoning common to all human beings, which they themselves call epilogismos. For instance, they say, when we mix together drugs that have shown the same action through experience, but one acted better or worse on one body and another on another, it seemed reasonable to us (so say some of the Empiricists, though certainly not all) to combine several such drugs, so that at least one of them might be found appropriate to the nature of the body under treatment. This, they say, is what happened in the case of this most novel drug called the 'cephalic', in which were mixed all the drugs known even singly to make flesh grow in ulcers. But by what reasoning verdigris mixed with simple cerate produces a flesh-producing drug, when neither of the ingredients mixed produces flesh, this they do not go on to say.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For verdigris eats away the 367 ulcerated flesh, being a hot and pungent drug; whereas the cerate is mild and non-biting, but instead of new flesh it breeds filth upon the ulcers. Greek (Kühn)

οὔκουν αὐταρκὲς ἐπίστασθαι θερμαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν τόδε τὸ 368 φάρμακον, ἀλλὰ καὶ μέχρι πόσου θερμαίνει καὶ ψύχει προσδιορίσασθαι χρή. θερμαίνουσι γὰρ αἱ ῥητίναι πᾶσαι καὶ τοῦτο κοινὸν αὐταῖς, ἀλλὰ τὸ μᾶλλον καὶ ἧττον οὐκ ὀλίγον ἐν τῇ θερμότητι κέκτηνται. θερμαίνουσι δὲ καὶ ὀποὶ πολλοὶ, διαφέροντες ἀλλήλων οὐ σμικρὰν διαφορὰν ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον. εἰ γὰρ παραβάλλοις ὀπὸν πάνακος τῷ Κυρηναίῳ καὶ Μηδικῷ, δόξει σοι τοῦτο μηδὲν ὡς πρὸς ἐκείνους εἶναι. καὶ τί δεῖ πόῤῥω τὸν λόγον ἀπάγειν, αὐτῶν οἷς ὁσημέραι προσφερόμεθα τῆς διαφορᾶς ἀναμνῆσαι δυναμένους; ἄνηθον γοῦν καὶ πήγανον καὶ κρόμμυον θερμαίνοντα πάντα πολὺ διενήνοχεν ἀλλήλων, ὥστε καὶ κατὰ τὰς θεραπείας τῷ μὲν ἱκανῶς ἐψυγμένῳ μορίῳ τὸ θερμαῖνον ἱκανῶς προσοίσομεν φάρμακον, τῷ δὲ βραχὺ τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν ἐξηλλαγμένῳ τὸ μετρίως θερμαῖνον, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τῷ πάνυ κατεψυγμένῳ τὸ σφοδρότατα θερμαῖνον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It is therefore not sufficient to know that this 368 drug heats or cools, but one must further determine to what degree it heats and cools. For all the resins heat, and this is common to them, but they possess the more and the less in heat to no small extent. Many juices also heat, differing from one another by no small difference in the more and the less. For if you were to compare the juice of panax with the Cyrenaic and the Median, this will seem to you to be nothing in comparison with those. And why need one carry the argument far afield, when we can recall the difference among the very things we take daily? Dill, at any rate, and rue and onion, though all of them heat, differ greatly from one another, so that in treatments too we shall apply to the part that has been considerably chilled the drug that heats considerably, to the part that has been altered only slightly from its natural state the drug that heats moderately, just as to the part that has been thoroughly chilled the drug that heats most vehemently.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Reasonably, then, we established four orders among the heating drugs, distinguishing from those that heat slightly those that do this clearly, those that heat very much, and those that heat intensely together with 369 burning. And in each of the four differentiae some drugs are, as it were, of the differentia as it begins, some of it as it ends and approaches the one after it, and some in between. At any rate, among the drugs that draw by means of heat, just as among those called septic, some display their own power as soon as they are applied to the body, while others require two or three or four hours; and some a whole day, or even the night after it, or even several in succession. These things, then, as in the case of heating drugs, so also in the case of cooling drugs, must be known by anyone who is going to compound a drug; and likewise also in the case of moistening and drying ones. The materials of drugs are some from plants, some from metals, and some from animals; and you have learned all of them, distinguished, in the treatise on simple drugs.

It was therefore reasonable that we established four orders among the heating drugs, distinguishing from those that heat to a slight degree those that do this clearly, those that heat very much, and those that heat intensely together with 369 burning. ἐμάθετε δὲ καὶ ὅτι πρῶτος σκοπὸς τῆς θεραπείας ἐκ τῶν νοσωδῶν εὑρίσκεται διαθέσεων, ὅτε τε συνενδείκνυται τούτοις ἡ τοῦ πεπονθότος μορίου φύσις, ἅμα τῇ τοῦ περιέχοντος ἡμᾶς ἀέρος κράσει καὶ κατάστασιν καὶ ἡλικίαν καὶ ὥραν καὶ χώραν, ἐπιτηδεύματά τε καὶ ἔθη συνεπισκέπτονται πολλοὶ τῶν ἀρίστων ἰατρῶν, 371 ὑπὲρ ὧν εἰρήσεταί τινα καὶ νῦν, ἔνθ’ ἂν αὐτῶν γένηται χρεία. ταῦτ’ οὖν ὑμᾶς οὕτως ἅπαντα διὰ μνήμης ἔχειν χρὴ πρόχειρα, βουλομένους ἐν ἅπαντι χωρίῳ φαρμάκων εὐπορεῖν ἁπλῶν τε καὶ συνθέτων, ἔτι τε πρὸς τούτοις, ὡς ἐνίοτε μὲν ἕν τι φάρμακον αὐτοφυὲς ἁπλοῦν, ἱκανόν ἐστι τὴν παρὰ φύσιν ἰάσασθαι διάθεσιν, ἐνίοτε δὲ χρεία γίγνεται συνθέτου καὶ διὰ τί γε πράττομεν τοῦτο λεκτέον ἐφεξῆς. [Τίς ἡ χρεία τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων.] Εἰ διὰ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων μόνον ἦν δυνατὸν ἁπάσας ἰάσασθαι τὰς παρὰ φύσιν διαθέσεις, οὐκ ἂν ἐδεήθημεν οὐδέποτε συνθέτου φαρμάκου, νυνὶ δὲ οὐχ οὕτως ἔχει. πολλάκις γὰρ ἄχρι τοσοῦδέ τινος ἐκθερμανθῆναι τὸ σῶμα βουλόμενοι τῶν ἁπλῶν οὐδὲν ἔχομεν τοιοῦτον. ἀνάλογον γὰρ εἶναι χρὴ τῇ θεραπευομένῃ διαθέσει τὸ θεραπεῦον φάρμακον. ἐὰν οὖν ἡ διάθεσις ᾖ ψυχρὰ, τέτταρσιν ἀριθμοῖς ἀφεστῶσα τῆς κατὰ φύσιν, ὅτι μὲν εἶναι χρὴ τὸ θεραπεῦσον αὐτὴν φάρμακον, ἴσῃ ποσότητι θερμότερον τοῦ συμμέτρου πρόδηλον παντί.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅτε τε, κατάστασιν, ἀριθμοῖς

You learned also that the first indication of the treatment is discovered from the morbid conditions, and that the nature of the affected part gives a joint indication together with these, along with the mixture of the air that surrounds us; and many of the best physicians consider in addition the constitution (of the atmosphere), age, season and region, as well as pursuits and habits, 371 about which some things will be said now too, wherever there is need of them. All these things, then, you must thus hold in memory ready to hand, if you wish in every place to be well supplied with drugs, both simple and compound; and further, in addition to these, that sometimes one single naturally occurring simple drug is sufficient to cure the condition contrary to nature, while sometimes there is need of a compound one; and why indeed we do this must be stated next. [What the use of compound drugs is.] If it were possible to cure all the conditions contrary to nature by means of simple drugs alone, we would never have needed a compound drug; but as it is, this is not the case. For often, wishing the body to be heated through up to a certain degree, we have no simple of that sort. For the drug that treats must be proportionate to the condition being treated. If, then, the condition is cold, standing removed from the natural one by four numbers, it is obvious to everyone that the drug which is to treat it must be hotter than the well-proportioned by an equal quantity.

You have learned, too, that the first goal of treatment is found from the diseased dispositions, and when the nature of the affected part co-indicates along with these. Together with the krasis of the air that surrounds us, many of the best physicians also take into consideration the state [of the weather], the age, the season and the region, as well as pursuits and habits, 371 about which some things will be said now too, wherever there is need of them. All these things, then, you must have thus ready at hand in memory, if you wish to be well supplied in every place with drugs both simple and compound. Further, in addition to these, you must bear in mind that sometimes a single natural, simple drug is sufficient to cure the disposition contrary to nature, while sometimes there is need of a compound one; and why indeed we do this must be stated next. [What the use of compound drugs is.] If it were possible to cure all the dispositions contrary to nature by means of simple drugs alone, we should never have needed a compound drug; but as it is, this is not so. For often, when we wish the body to be heated through to such and such a degree, we have no simple drug of that kind. For the drug that treats must be proportionate to the disposition being treated. If, then, the disposition is cold, standing four numbers away from the natural state, it is evident to everyone that the drug which is to treat it must be hotter than the moderate by an equal quantity.

Greek (Kühn)

﻿ 372 μὴ παρόντος δ’ ἐνίοτε μηδενὸς τοιούτου τῶν ἁπλῶν ἂν εὐπορῶμεν δυοῖν, ὧν τὸ μὲν ἀριθμοῖς πέντε, τὸ δὲ τρισὶ θερμότερον, μίξαντες αὐτὰ τὸ μέσον ἐργασόμεθα, τέτρασιν ἀριθμοῖς ὑπερέχον τοῦ συμμέτρου. τινὰ δὲ τῶν ἁπλῶν οὐδ’ εἰς χρῆσιν ἀχθῆναι δύναται χωρὶς ἑτέρας μίξεως, οἷον ὅταν ἐμπλαστὸν φάρμακον ἐπιτιθέναι τινὶ βουλώμεθα. τῶν γὰρ ἁπλῶν οὐδὲν ἐπιτήδειον εἰς τὴν τοιαύτην χρείαν. οὔτ’ οὖν ἄσφαλτος οὔτε κηρὸς οὔτε ῥητίνη, καθάπερ οὐδὲ πίσσα πρόπολίς τε καὶ λάδανον, ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον ἰὸς καὶ ψιμύθιον ἢ λιθάργυρος ἢ μολύβδαινα ἢ τῶν ἄλλων τι με ταλλικῶν φαρμάκων. ὡσαύτως οὐδὲ βοτάναι μόναι φάρμακον ἐμπλαστὸν δύνανται ποιεῖν. εἰκότως οὖν ἐπενόησαν οἱ πρώτως συνθέντες ἐμπλαστὰ φάρμακα καὶ δι’ ἐλαίου μίξεως ἕψειν μὲν τὰ μεταλλικὰ, τήκειν δὲ τὰ τηκτὰ, προσεμβάλλειν δ’ αὐτοῖς καὶ βοτάνας ξηρὰς, κεκομμένας τε καὶ διηθημένας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μολύβδαινα, με ταλλικῶν

372 But when sometimes no such simple is available, if we have at hand two, of which the one is hotter by five degrees and the other by three, by mixing them we shall produce the mean, which exceeds the well-tempered state by four degrees. Some of the simples, moreover, cannot even be brought into use without mixture with something else, as for instance when we wish to apply an emplastic drug to someone; for none of the simples is suitable for such a use. Neither asphalt, then, nor wax nor resin, just as neither pitch nor propolis nor ladanum, and still less verdigris and white lead or litharge or molybdaena or any of the other metallic drugs. In the same way neither can herbs by themselves make an emplastic drug. Reasonably, therefore, those who first compounded emplastic drugs devised the plan of boiling the metallic substances with an admixture of oil, of melting the meltable ones, and of adding to them also dry herbs, pounded and sifted.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀριθμοῖς, συμμέτρου, με ταλλικῶν, μολύβδαινα, διηθημένας

372 But since sometimes no such simple is at hand, if we have a supply of two, of which the one is hotter by five degrees and the other by three, by mixing them we shall produce the mean, exceeding the moderate by four degrees. Some of the simples, moreover, cannot even be brought into use without a further admixture, as when we wish to apply a plaster drug to someone. For none of the simples is suitable for such a use: neither bitumen nor wax nor resin, just as neither pitch, propolis and ladanum, and still more verdigris and white lead or litharge or molybdaena or any other of the metallic drugs. Likewise herbs alone cannot make a plaster drug either. Reasonably, therefore, those who first compounded plaster drugs devised, through the admixture of oil, to boil the metallic substances and to melt the meltable ones, and to throw in with them also dry herbs, pounded and sifted.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐφ’ ἑτέρων πάλιν διαθέσεων, χρεία ἑνὸς φαρμάκου μόνου τῶν αὐτοφυῶν ἐστιν, ἐπιμίγνυμεν δ’ αὐτῷ τινὰ, ποτὲ μὲν 373 ἀμβλῦναι τὸ σφοδρὸν τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ βουλευόμενοι, ποτὲ δὲ τὴν ἀηδίαν παραμυθήσασθαι, καθάπερ Ἱπποκράτης ἐν τῷ περὶ διαίτης ὀξέων ἔφη κατὰ τήνδε τὴν ῥῆσιν· ἢν δ’ ὑπὸ φρένας ᾖ τὸ ἄλγημα, ἐς δὲ τὴν κληῗδα μὴ σημήνῃ, μαλθάσσειν χρὴ τὴν κοιλίην ἢ μέλανι ἐλλεβόρῳ ἢ πεπλίῳ, μέλανι μὲν δαῦκον ἢ σέσελι ἢ κύμινον ἢ ἄνισον ἢ ἄλλο τι τῶν εὐωδέων μίσγοντα, πεπλίῳ δὲ ὀπὸν σιλφίου. ἐν ταύτῃ τῇ ῥήσει σαφῶς ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐνεδείξατο χρείαν μὲν εἶναι τοῦ καθαίροντος φαρμάκου, τὴν δ’ ἀηδίαν αὐτοῦ κατὰ τὴν πόσιν καὶ τὴν ἐν τῇ κοιλίᾳ μόνην ἡδύσματος δεῖσθαι. πολλὰ γάρ τοι τῶν πινομένων φαρμάκων ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον ἀηδῆ τοῖς προσφερομένοις ἐστὶν, ὡς ἀνατρέπεσθαι τὸν στόμαχον, ἐμεῖσθαί τε παραχρῆμα, τινὰ δὲ κᾂν ἐπ’ ὀλίγον ἐν τῇ γαστρὶ μεῖναι δυνηθῇ, μετὰ ταῦτα γοῦν ἐμεῖσθαι μοχθηρὰς ἐρυγὰς κινήσαντα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

In other conditions, again, there is need of one single drug from among the naturally occurring ones, but we mix something in with it, sometimes 373 intending to blunt the vehemence of its power, sometimes to mitigate its unpleasantness, just as Hippocrates said in the work On Regimen in Acute Diseases in the following passage: 'If the pain is beneath the diaphragm and gives no sign toward the collarbone, one must soften the belly either with black hellebore or with peplion, mixing with the black (hellebore) daucus or seseli or cumin or anise or some other of the fragrant things, and with peplion the juice of silphium.' In this passage Hippocrates clearly indicated that there is need of the purging drug, but that its unpleasantness in the drinking and in the belly alone requires a sweetening agent. For indeed many of the drugs that are drunk are so unpleasant to those who take them that the stomach is upset and they are vomited at once, while some, even if they are able to remain for a short time in the belly, are nonetheless afterwards vomited, having stirred up foul eructations.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μόνην, σημήνῃ, πεπλίῳ

In other conditions, again, there is need of a single drug only, one of the natural ones, but we mix something in with it, sometimes 373 intending to blunt the vehemence of its power, sometimes to mitigate its unpleasantness, as Hippocrates said in On Regimen in Acute Diseases in the following passage: 'If the pain is below the diaphragm and gives no sign towards the collarbone, one must soften the belly either with black hellebore or with peplion; with the black, mixing in daukos or seseli or cumin or anise or some other of the fragrant things, and with the peplion, juice of silphium.' In this passage Hippocrates clearly indicated that there is need of the purging drug, but that only its unpleasantness, in the drinking and in the belly, requires a seasoning. For indeed many of the drugs that are drunk are so unpleasant to those who take them that the stomach is upset and they are vomited at once; and some, even if they are able to remain in the belly for a little while, are at any rate vomited afterwards, having provoked noxious belchings.

Greek (Kühn)

καθάπερ δὲ τῶν καθαιρόντων οἷον ἡδύσματά ἐστι τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν φαρμάκων, ὧν Ἱπποκράτης ἐμνημόνευσεν, οὕτως τῶν ἀνωδύνων ὀνομαζομένων, ἕτερα τῆς σφοδρότητος ὑπάρχει πραϋντικὰ, καθάπερ ὅταν ὀπὸν μήκωνος 374 ἢ ὑοσκυάμου σπέρμα παραδιδόαμεν ἢ μανδραγόρου ῥίζης φλοιὸν, ὥστε καὶ αὕτη χρεία συνθέσεως φαρμάκων ἐστί. μεγίστη δ’ ἐπὶ τῶν ἐναντίων δυνάμεων ἅμα χρῃζόντων παθῶν, οἷον ἀποκρουστικῶν τε καὶ διαφορητικῶν ἢ ῥυπτικῶν τε καὶ λεαντικῶν, ἢ παχυνόντων τε ἅμα καὶ λεπτυνόντων χυμοὺς, καθά τε πολυχρηστότατα καὶ κάλλιστα φάρμακα τὰς ἐναντίας ἐν αὐτοῖς ἔχει δυνάμεις, ὡς ἐπιδείξομεν. ἀλλὰ καὶ ἥδε ἡ χρεία συνθέσεως φαρμάκων ἐστὶν, ὅταν ἕν τι φάρμακον ἔχειν βουλώμεθα πρὸς πολλὰ τῶν ἰοβόλων θηρίων ἢ θανασίμων φαρμάκων ἐπιτήδειον, ἥτις χρεία καὶ τὴν θηριακὴν ὀνομαζομένην ἀντίδοτον ἐσκεύασε καὶ πρὸς αὐτῇ τὴν Μιθριδάτειον ἑτέρας τε πολλάς. εἰσὶ δὲ καὶ ἄλλαι τινὲς χρεῖαι, μικρότεραι μὲν τῶν εἰρημένων, ὅμως δ’ οὖν ἀναγκάζουσαι πολλάκις ἡμᾶς ἐπὶ τὴν σύνθεσιν ἀφικνεῖσθαι τῶν φαρμάκων, ἃς ἐν τῇδε τῇ πραγματείᾳ πάσας μαθήσεσθε. νυνὶ γὰρ ἤδη τῶν προκειμένων ἄρξομαι, πρώτου μνημονεύσας ἐμπλαστοῦ φαρμάκου πολυχρηστοτάτου συντεθέντος ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παραδιδόαμεν, καθά τε

And just as, for the purgatives, such drugs as those which Hippocrates mentioned are, as it were, sweeteners, so too for the drugs called anodynes there are others that mitigate their vehemence, as when we administer poppy juice 374 or henbane seed or the bark of mandrake root; so that this too is a need for the composition of drugs. But the greatest need is in the case of affections that require opposite powers at the same time, such as repellent and discutient, or detergent and smoothing, or thickening and at the same time thinning of humours; and accordingly the most widely useful and the best drugs have the opposite powers in them, as we shall show. But there is also this need for the composition of drugs, when we wish to have a single drug suitable against many of the venomous beasts or deadly drugs—a need which produced the antidote called Theriac, and in addition to it the Mithridatium and many others. There are also certain other needs, lesser than those mentioned, but nevertheless often compelling us to have recourse to the composition of drugs, all of which you will learn in this treatise. For now I shall at once begin on the matters proposed, mentioning first a plaster drug of the widest usefulness composed by me.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθά τε

Just as such drugs as Hippocrates mentioned are, as it were, seasonings of the purgatives, so for the drugs called anodyne there are other things that mitigate their violence, as when we give poppy juice 374 or henbane seed or the bark of mandrake root; so this too is a use for the compounding of drugs. The greatest use, however, is in the case of affections that require opposite powers at the same time, for instance repellent and discutient, or detergent and smoothing, or at once thickening and thinning the humours; and accordingly the most versatile and finest drugs contain the opposite powers within themselves, as we shall show. But this too is a use for the compounding of drugs, when we wish to have a single drug suitable against many venomous beasts or deadly poisons; it is this use that produced the antidote called theriac, and besides it the Mithridatic and many others. There are also certain other uses, smaller than those mentioned, which nevertheless often compel us to resort to the compounding of drugs; all of these you will learn in this treatise. For now I shall at once begin on the matters before us, first mentioning a plaster drug of very wide use compounded by me.

375 [Περὶ τοῦ διὰ χαλκίτεως ἐμπλαστοῦ φαρμάκου.] Τίνι λόγῳ συνέθηκα τὴν διὰ χαλκίτεως ἔμπλαστρον, ἣν φοινικίνην ὀνομάζω, κολλητικήν τε καὶ συνουλωτικὴν ἑλκῶν καὶ φλεγμονῶν θεραπευτικὴν, ἐπειδὰν τακεῖσα σύστασιν ὑγρᾶς ἔχῃ κηρωτῆς, τοὺς μὲν ἐμπειρικοὺς ἐάσαντες ὁμολογεῖν τὴν ἑαυτῶν ἀμαθίαν, ἀποστάντες δὲ καὶ τῶν περὶ τὸν Θεσσαλὸν, ἀγνοούντων τὰ κάλλιστα τῶν ἡυρημένων τοῖς ἰατροῖς φαρμάκων, ἐξ ἐναντίων δυνάμεων συγκείμενα, τὸν λογισμὸν ἀσκεῖτε πρὸς τὸ δύνασθαι συντιθέναι φάρμακα ταῖς αὐταῖς ἐμοὶ χρώμενοι μεθόδοις, ἵν’ ἔργῳ καὶ αὐτοὶ πεισθῆτε καὶ τὰ τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν ἡυρημένα λογισμῷ συντίθεσθαι πάντα, ληρώδους τῆς τῶν ἐμπειρικῶν περιπτώσεως οὔσης. λεκτέον οὖν ἤδη τὸν λόγον τῆς τοῦ προκειμένου φαρμάκου συνθέσεως. διαφορητικὴν μὲν ἔχει δύναμιν ἔλαιόν τε παλαιὸν καὶ στέαρ, ἀποκρουστικὴν δὲ καὶ στυπτικὴν ἥ τε χαλκῖτις καὶ ὁ τοῦ φοίνικος χυλός.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

375 [On the plaster-drug made with chalcitis.] As to the reasoning by which I composed the plaster made with chalcitis, which I call phoinikine (the date-plaster) — agglutinative of wounds and cicatrising them, and curative of inflammations, whenever, once melted, it has the consistency of a liquid cerate — leave the Empiricists to confess their own ignorance, and stand apart also from the followers of Thessalus, who are ignorant of the finest of the drugs discovered by physicians, those composed out of opposite powers; and exercise your reasoning so as to be able to compose drugs, using the same methods as I do, in order that you yourselves may be persuaded by actual practice that the drugs discovered by our predecessors were all composed by reasoning, the 'chance encounter' of the Empiricists being nonsense. So now the rationale of the composition of the drug before us must be stated. Old oil and fat have a discutient power, while chalcitis and the juice of the date have a repellent and astringent power.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἑλκῶν, στέαρ

375 [On the plaster drug made with chalcitis.] By what reasoning did I compound the plaster made with chalcitis, which I call the palm plaster (phoinikinē), an agglutinative and cicatrizing remedy for wounds (helkē), and curative of inflammations whenever, once melted, it has the consistency of a liquid cerate? You, leaving the Empiricists to confess their own ignorance, and standing apart also from the followers of Thessalus, who are ignorant of the finest of the drugs discovered by physicians, which are composed of contrary powers, must train your reasoning so that you are able to compound drugs using the same methods as I do. Then you yourselves may be persuaded by the work itself that everything discovered by our predecessors was also compounded by reasoning, the accidental encounter (periptōsis) of the Empiricists being a trifling thing. I must now, then, set out the reasoning behind the composition of the drug before us. Old olive oil and fat have a discutient power, while chalcitis and the juice of the palm have a repellent and astringent power.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐκ τούτων οὖν ἐγὼ συνέθηκα φάρμακον, ᾧ πάνυ πολλοὺς ἤδη χρωμένους 376 ἴστε διὰ τὸ τῶν ὁμοειδῶν ἁπάντων ἄμεινον κεκρίσθαι, δι’ αὐτῶν τῶν ἔργων βασανισθέν. ὁ μὲν γὰρ λόγος εὑρίσκει τὴν σύνθεσιν, ἡ δὲ πεῖρα κρίνει τὴν τῶν εὑρεθέντων ἀρετὴν, ὥστε καὶ τοῖς ἀπείροις λογικῶν μεθόδων πιστεύεσθαι τὰ τεχνικῶς συντεθέντα. προσέμιξα δὲ τοῖς εἰρημένοις ἄρτι φαρμάκοις λιθάργυρον οὐδέν τι μέγα συντελέσαι δυναμένην οὔτε τοῖς ἀναστέλλουσι τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον οὔτε τοῖς διαφοροῦσι τὸ ἐστηριγμένον ἤδη κατὰ τὰ φλεγμαίνοντα μόρια· ξηραντικῆς γάρ ἐστι δυνάμεως οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρᾶς, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἐπιτήδειον φάρμακον αὐτὴν εἰς ἐμπλαστοῦ σύνθεσιν ἔμιξα, σὺν γὰρ τοῖς εἰρημένοις ἕψηται μέχρι συστάσεως ἀμολύντου. χρὴ δὲ τὸ στέαρ ἄναλόν τε καὶ ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα παλαιότατον εἶναι, τό τε ἔλαιον ὁμοίως παλαιόν. ἐμάθετε γάρ πως παραπλησίας μὲν εἶναι ἀλλήλοις δυνάμεως τό τε ἔλαιον καὶ τὸ στέαρ τῶν ὑῶν, μαλακτικώτερον βραχὺ καὶ θερμότερον ὂν τῇ δυνάμει τὸ στέαρ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

From these, then, I composed a drug which you know very many people already use, 376 because it has been judged better than all those of the same kind, having been tested by the works themselves. For reason discovers the composition, but experience judges the excellence of what has been discovered, so that things composed with art are trusted even by those inexperienced in rational methods. And I mixed in with the drugs just mentioned litharge, which is able to contribute nothing great either to those that repel what flows in or to those that disperse what is already fixed in the inflamed parts; for it is of a drying faculty, yet not a strong one; but I mixed it in as a drug suitable for the composition of a plaster, for it is boiled together with the aforesaid until a consistency that does not stain. The fat must be unsalted and as old as possible, and the oil likewise old. For you have learned, I think, that oil and the fat of pigs are of a faculty similar to one another, the fat being slightly more emollient and hotter in its faculty.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐμάθετε γάρ πως

From these, then, I compounded a drug which you know very many people are already 376 using, because it has been judged better than all others of the same kind, having been tested by the results themselves. For reason discovers the composition, but experience judges the excellence of what has been discovered, so that even those inexperienced in logical methods come to trust what has been compounded according to the art. To the drugs just mentioned I added litharge, which cannot contribute anything of great consequence either to those that check what flows in or to those that disperse what is already fixed in the inflamed parts; for it is of a drying faculty, though not a strong one. Rather, I mixed it in as a drug suited to the composition of a plaster, for it is boiled together with the aforementioned until it reaches a non-soiling consistency. The fat should be unsalted and as old as possible, and the oil likewise old. For you have learned, I think, that oil and swine's fat are of a faculty close to one another, the fat being slightly more emollient and hotter in faculty.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐμάθετε δὲ καὶ ὡς παλαιούμενα λεπτομερέστερά τε καὶ διαφορητικώτερα γίγνεται καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ταῖς μὲν ὀδυνηραῖς φλεγμοναῖς παρηγορικώτερα ﻿ 377 τὰ νέα, ταῖς δὲ μὴ τοιαύταις ὠφελιμώτερα τὰ παλαιά. καὶ ὅσῳ γ’ ἂν ᾖ χρονιωτέρα τε καὶ δύσλυτος ἡ φλεγμονὴ, τοσούτῳ τὰ παλαιὰ χρησιμώτερα καὶ μόνα θεραπεύειν ἱκανὰ μηδενὸς ἐπιῤῥέοντος ἔτι τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ. εἰ δ’ ἐπιῤῥέοι τι, μικτὴν εἶναι χρὴ καὶ τοῦ φαρμάκου τὴν δύναμιν, οἵαπερ ἐστὶ τοῦ συντεθέντος ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ. διὰ τοῦτο γοῦν ἔμιξα τοῖς διαφοροῦσι τὴν χαλκῖτιν καὶ τὸν φοίνικα, παντάπασιν ὀλίγας φλεγμονὰς εἰδὼς χρονιζούσας, ἐφ’ ὧν οὐδὲν ἐπιῤῥεῖ. διὰ γὰρ τοὺς ἐπιῤῥέοντας αὐτοῖς χυμοὺς εἰώθασι χρονίζειν. ἀρχομένων δὲ καὶ αὐξανομένων τῶν φλεγμονῶν ἐπικρατεῖν προσήκει τὴν τῶν στυφόντων τε καὶ ψυχόντων φαρμάκων δύναμιν, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τηκόμενον εἰς πάχος κηρωτῆς ὑγρᾶς τὸ ἐμπλαστὸν τοῦτο φάρμακον ἱκανῶς πολύχρηστον γίγνεται ποτὲ μὲν οἴνου προσλαμβάνον ἢ ὄξους ἢ ὕδατος, ἔστι δὲ ὅτε δι’ ὠμοτριβοῦς ἐλαίου τηκόμενον ἤ τινος τῶν στύψιν ἐχόντων, οἷόν ἐστι τὸ Σπανόν τε καὶ τὸ Ἰστρικὸν, ἢ διά τινος τῶν γλυκέων ἄνευ στύψεως ἢ διὰ παλαιοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

You also learned that as they grow old they become finer in their parts and more discutient, and for this reason the new ones are more soothing for painful inflammations, 377 while the old ones are more beneficial for those that are not such. And the more chronic and hard to resolve the inflammation is, the more useful are the old ones, and they alone are sufficient to cure it, when nothing is any longer flowing into the affected part. But if something is flowing in, the power of the drug too must be mixed, such as is that of the one composed by me. For this reason, at any rate, I mixed chalcitis and palm with the discutient drugs, knowing that there are altogether few inflammations that become chronic in which nothing flows in; for it is because of the humours flowing into them that they are accustomed to become chronic. But when inflammations are beginning and increasing, the power of the astringent and cooling drugs ought to predominate, and for this reason, when melted down to the thickness of a liquid cerate, this emplastic drug becomes quite a polychreston (of many uses), sometimes taking in addition wine or vinegar or water, and sometimes being melted with oil of unripe olives (omotribes) or with one of those that have astringency, such as the Spanish and the Istrian, or with one of the sweet ones without astringency, or with old oil.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὠμοτριβοῦς, φοίνικα, τοῖς διαφοροῦσι, Ἰστρικὸν

You have also learned that as they grow old they become finer in their parts and more discutient, and that for this reason the new ones are more soothing for painful inflammations 377, while for those that are not of this kind the old ones are more beneficial. And the more chronic and hard to resolve the inflammation is, the more useful the old ones are, and they alone are sufficient to treat it, provided that nothing is still flowing into the affected part. When inflammations are beginning and increasing, the power of astringent and cooling drugs ought to predominate; and for this reason this plaster drug, melted down to the consistency of a liquid cerate, becomes sufficiently polychrest, sometimes taking in addition wine or vinegar or water, and at other times being melted with omotribes oil, or with one of those oils that have astringency, such as the Spanish and the Istrian, or with one of the sweet oils without astringency, or with old oil.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ 378 διὰ μυρσίνου δὲ καὶ μηλίνου πολλάκις ἔτηξα τὸ φάρμακον, ἐφ’ ὧν ἐστι στύψεως ἰσχυροτέρας χρεία. αὕτη μὲν οὖν ἡ σύνθεσις τοῦ φαρμάκου κατὰ τοιάνδε γίγνεται συμμετρίαν. τοῦ παλαιοῦ στέατος ὃ κα λοῦσιν ἀξούγγιον οἱ πιπράσκοντες ἔστωσαν λίτραι β΄. τοῦ δὲ ἐλαίου τοῦ παλαιοῦ λίτραι γ΄. καὶ λιθαργύρου λίτραι γ΄. χαλκίτεώς τε οὐγγίαι δ΄. ἕψειν δὲ αὐτὰ χρὴ κατὰ τόνδε τὸν τρόπον. τοῦ στέατος τοὺς ὑμένας ἐξελὼν καὶ τὸ λοιπὸν θλάσας, ὡς εὔτηκτον γενέσθαι μετὰ τοῦτο τῆξον ἐπὶ πυρὸς, εἶτα λαβὼν ἠθμὸν διήθησον, ὡς καθαρὸν αὐτὸ γενέσθαι, μηδὲν ὑμένος ἔχον, ἕλκον σταθμὸν τὰς προειρημένας δύο λίτρας, εἶτα λελειωμένας τὰς δ΄. οὐγγίας τῆς χαλκίτεως σὺν ταῖς τρισὶ λίτρας τοῦ ἐλαίου μίξεις τῷ στέατι, προσεπιβαλὼν καὶ τὰς γ΄. λίτρας τῆς λιθαργύρου, καὶ πάντα κατὰ θυείαν ἑνώσας ἔμβαλλε ἐν τῇ κακκάβῃ, κινῶν ξύλῳ φοίνικος. ἀρκέσει δέ σοι κλάδος εἷς τῶν μεγάλων, ὡς τῷ μὲν ἁδρῷ μέρει τοῦ ξύλου χρῆσθαι κινοῦντα, κατὰ τὴν τῆς καλουμένης σπάθης χρείαν· οὕτω γὰρ ὀνομάζουσιν ᾧ κινοῦσι τὰ σκευαζόμενα, καὶ ἡμεῖς τοῦ σαφοῦς ἐχόμενοι καλέσομεν ὡσαύτως αὐτοῖς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἕλκον σταθμὸν

And 378 I have often melted the drug in myrtle oil and in quince oil, in those cases where there is need of a stronger astringency. This composition of the drug, then, is made according to the following proportion. Of the old fat, which those who sell it call axoungion, let there be 2 litrai; of the old oil, 3 litrai; and of litharge, 3 litrai; and of chalcitis, 4 ounces. One must boil them in the following manner. Having removed the membranes of the fat and crushed the remainder so that it becomes easy to melt, after this melt it over a fire, then take a strainer and strain it, so that it becomes pure, containing nothing of membrane, drawing in weight the aforesaid two litrai; then you will mix the 4 ounces of chalcitis, finely ground, together with the three litrai of oil, with the fat, adding in also the 3 litrai of litharge, and having united everything in a mortar, put it into the cauldron, stirring with a stick of palm-wood. A single branch of the large ones will suffice for you, so as to use the thick part of the wood for stirring, in the function of the so-called spathe; for thus they name that with which they stir the things being prepared, and we, holding to clarity, shall call it the same as they do.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κα λοῦσιν (read as καλοῦσιν), ἕλκον σταθμὸν

And 378 I have often also melted the drug down with myrtle oil and quince oil, in cases where there is need of a stronger astringency. This, then, is the composition of the drug, made up in the following proportion. Of old fat, which the sellers call axungia (axoungion), let there be 2 litrai; of old oil, 3 litrai; of litharge, 3 litrai; and of chalcitis, 4 ounces. They must be boiled in the following manner. Take out the membranes of the fat and crush the rest, so that it becomes easily melted. After this, melt it over a fire. Then take a strainer and strain it, so that it becomes clean, with nothing of membrane in it, and weighing the aforesaid two litrai. Then you will mix the 4 ounces of chalcitis, ground smooth, together with the three litrai of oil, into the fat, adding also the 3 litrai of litharge. When you have united everything in a mortar, put it into the cooking-pot (kakkabē), stirring with a stick of palm wood. One of the large branches will suffice you, so that you use the thick part of the wood for stirring, serving the purpose of the so-called spatha. For that is the name they give to the implement with which they stir the things being prepared, and we too, holding to what is clear, shall call it the same as they do.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ 379 δὲ συνεχὲς τῷ ἁδρῷ μέρει τοῦ κλάδου περικαθῄρας καὶ τὰς ἑκατέρωθεν ἐκφύσεις τὰς λεπτὰς ἀποτρίψας κατάτεμνε, προπεριξύσας τὸν φλοιὸν, ὅπως ἐμβληθέντα τὰ τμήματα τῷ φαρμάκῳ μεταδῷ τοῦ καθ’ ἑαυτὰ χυλοῦ. τῇ μὲν οὖν σπάθῃ χρῆσθαι προσῆκεν ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς εὐθέως, τὰ δὲ λεπτὰ τμήματα τῆς φοίνικος ἐμβάλλειν, ὅταν εἰς σύστασιν ἥκῃ τὸ φάρμακον ὁμοίαν κηρωτῇ. ἐὰν γὰρ ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς ἐμβάλλῃς αὐτὰ, καταναλωθήσεται διὰ τῆς ἑψήσεως ὁ χυλὸς τῶν ἐμβληθέντων, ὃν διαμεῖναι βουλόμεθα. καὶ αὐτῆς δὲ τῆς σπάθης, ᾗ κινεῖς ἐξ ἀρχῆς τὸ φάρμακον, ὅταν τὸ διαβρεχόμενον ἐν τῇ κακκάβῃ γένηται ξηρὸν, ὡς μηκέτ’ ἔχειν τὸν οἰκεῖον χυλὸν, ἀποτέμνων αὐτὸ καὶ ἀποῤῥίπτων τῷ λοιπῷ χρῷ. καὶ αὖθις ὁμοίως εἰ τύχοι τὸ διαβρεχόμενον ἐν τῷ φαρμάκῳ ξηρανθὲν αὖθις ἀπότεμνε. πρόδηλον δ’ ὅτι τὸν κλάδον τοῦ φοίνικος οὐ χρὴ πρὸ πολλῶν ἡμερῶν ἀποτετμῆσθαι τοῦ δένδρου, ξηραίνεται γὰρ ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ καὶ ἄχυλος γίνεται. κατὰ τὴν προτέραν οὖν, εἴπερ εἴη χειμὼν, ἀφῃρήσθω, θέρους δὲ χρὴ καὶ κατ’ αὐτὴν ἐκείνην τὴν ἡμέραν, ἐν ᾗπερ ἂν ἕψῃς τὸ φάρμακον, ἀφῃρῆσθαι τοῦ δένδρου τὸν κλάδον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But the part 379 continuous with the thick part of the branch you should clean all around, and, having rubbed off the thin outgrowths on either side, cut it up, having first scraped off the bark all round, so that the pieces, when thrown into the drug, may impart to it the juice within themselves. Now it is proper to use the spatula straightaway from the beginning, but to throw in the thin pieces of the palm when the drug has come to a consistency like that of a cerate. For if you throw them in from the beginning, the juice of what has been thrown in, which we wish to remain, will be consumed through the boiling. And as for the spatula itself, with which you stir the drug from the beginning, when the part that is being soaked in the cauldron becomes dry, so that it no longer has its own juice, cut it off and throw it away and use the remainder. And again likewise, if it should happen that the part being soaked in the drug has dried out, cut it off again. It is evident that the branch of the palm must not have been cut from the tree many days beforehand, for in time it dries out and becomes juiceless. So, if it is winter, let it be removed on the previous day; but in summer the branch must be removed from the tree on that very day on which you boil the drug.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκφύσεις, περικαθῄρας

379 The part continuous with the thick part of the branch you should clean all round, rub off the thin outgrowths on either side, and cut it up, having first scraped off the bark, so that the pieces, when thrown in, may impart to the drug the juice they contain. The spatula, then, should be used right from the beginning, but the thin pieces of the palm should be thrown in when the drug comes to a consistency like that of a cerate. For if you throw them in from the beginning, the juice of what has been thrown in, which we want to remain, will be used up in the boiling. And as for the spatula itself, with which you stir the drug from the beginning, when the part being soaked in the pot becomes dry, so as no longer to have its own juice, cut that part off, throw it away, and use the rest. And again likewise, if the part being soaked in the drug happens to have dried, cut it off again. It is evident that the palm branch must not have been cut from the tree many days beforehand, for it dries out in time and becomes juiceless. So if it is winter, let it be removed on the day before; but in summer the branch must be removed from the tree on that very day on which you boil the drug.

ὅταν δ’ οὕτως 380 ἑψόμενον ἀμόλυντον γένηται τὸ φάρμακον, αὐτῷ μὲν ἐμπλαστῷ χρήσῃ κατά τε τῶν δυσεπουλώτων ἑλκῶν καὶ τῶν ἐναίμων ἐλαίῳ διατήκων, ἐπὶ τῶν φλεγμαινόντων, ἐν ποδάγραις, ἐν ἀρθρίτισιν, ἐν βουβῶσιν, ἐν φύμασιν, ἐν κατακαύμασιν, ἐν χειμέθλοις, ἐν κατάγμασιν, ἐν θλάσμασιν, ἐν ταῖς ῥευματικαῖς καλουμέναις διαθέσεσιν ἁπάσαις. ἤδη δὲ καὶ τῶν τὰς κήλας τεμνόντων πολλοὶ μετὰ τὴν χειρουργίαν εὐθέως ἐπιτιθέασιν αὐτὸ, μετά τε τῶν ἐπιβροχῶν καὶ τῶν καταπλασμάτων διαπραττόμενοι. εἰ μέντοι τῆς χαλκίτεως ἐμβάλοις οὐγγίας στ΄. ὅπερ ἐστὶν ἥμισυ λίτρας, ἔσται σοι μεγάλων τραυμάτων κολλητικώτερον τὸ φάρμακον ἐπουλωτικώτερόν τε τῶν δυσεπουλώτων. ἀλλ’ ἐν τούτῳ μέν ἐστι καὶ ἄλλα φάρμακα τῆς αὐτῆς δυνάμεως. τηχθέντι δὲ αὐτῷ μετ’ ἐλαίου θαῤῥεῖν χρὴ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων μᾶλλον ἐπὶ τῶν ῥευματικῶν διαθέσεων. ἐν γοῦν τῇ Ῥώμῃ πλείστων γιγνομένων τοιούτων, οὐδὲν οὕτως ηὐδοκίμησεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀμόλυντον, διαπραττόμενοι

But when the drug, being boiled in this way, 380 has become non-staining (ἀμόλυντον), you will use it as a plaster both upon ulcers that are hard to cicatrize and upon fresh bleeding wounds; and, melting it down with oil, upon inflamed parts, in podagra, in arthritis, in buboes, in phymata, in burns, in chilblains, in fractures, in contusions, and in all the so-called rheumatic conditions. Indeed, many of those who operate on hernias also apply it immediately after the surgery, carrying through the treatment with it together with the affusions and the poultices. If, however, you add six ounces of chalcitis, which is half a pound, the drug will be for you more agglutinative of large wounds and more cicatrizing of ulcers that are hard to cicatrize. But in this respect there are also other drugs of the same power; whereas, when it has been melted with oil, one should trust it more than all the others in the rheumatic conditions. At any rate in Rome, where very many such conditions occur, nothing has won such approval.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀμόλυντον, ἐναίμων, διαπραττόμενοι

When the drug, boiled 380 in this way, has become non-staining, you will use it by itself as a plaster on ulcers that are hard to cicatrise and on bloody ones; dissolved in oil, on inflamed parts, in podagra, in arthritis, in buboes, in phymata, in burns, in chilblains, in fractures, in contusions, and in all the so-called fluxional conditions. Indeed, many of those who cut hernias apply it immediately after the operation, carrying on the treatment with embrocations and cataplasms. If, however, you add six ounces of chalcitis, which is half a litra, the drug will be more agglutinative of large wounds and more cicatrising of ulcers that are hard to cicatrise. In this respect, however, there are other drugs of the same power too. But when it has been melted with oil, one should rely on it more than on all the others in fluxional conditions. At any rate, in Rome, where very many such cases occur, nothing has been so highly esteemed.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπιτήδειον δὲ, ὡς ἔφην, ἔστι καὶ ποδάγραις καὶ ἀρθρίτισιν ἐν ἅπαντι καιρῷ, 381 καθ’ ὃν οὐκ εἰσὶν ὀδύναι σφοδραὶ, δεόμεναι καταντλημάτων τε καὶ καταπλασμάτων παρηγορικῶν. ἀρχομένων οὖν καὶ αὐξομένων ἔτι τῶν τοιούτων διαθέσεως, ἐπειδὰν τήξῃς τὸ φάρμακον, ἐάσας ἀποψυχθῆναι, καὶ ξύων σπαθομήλῃ τὸ ξυσθὲν ἔμβαλε εἰς θυείαν παραχέων οἴνου καὶ τρίβων ταῖς χερσὶ πρὸς τῇ θυείᾳ, χάριν τοῦ δέξασθαι τὸν οἶνον. ἔστω δ’ οὗτος ἐκ τῶν αὐστηροτέρων, ἡλικίαν μὲν μέσος, οὐ παχὺς δὲ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν, ἀλλ’ ὡς οἷόν τε μάλιστα διαυγέστερος. ὁ γὰρ τοιοῦτος, ὡς λεπτομερέστερος ὢν, εἰς τὸ βάθος δύεται τῶν σωμάτων. ἄχρι τοσούτου δ’ αὐτὸν ἐπίχει τῷ τετηκότι φαρμάκῳ, τρίβων ταῖς χερσὶν ἄχρις ἂν ὑποδέχηται καὶ μιγνύηται καὶ μὴ παραπλέῃ τὸ ὑγρὸν ἄμικτον τῷ τριβομένῳ τε καὶ μαλασσομένῳ φαρμάκῳ. καὶ χωρὶς δὲ τοῦ προψύχοντα ξύειν ἔτι θερμῷ τῷ τετηκότι φαρμάκῳ τὸν οἶνον ἐγχέας τις ἀναμῖξαι δυνήσεται. κατὰ μὲν οὖν τὰς ἀρχὰς τῶν φλεγμονῶν τὴν ἀποκρουστικὴν δύναμιν ἐπικρατεῖν προσήκει, κατὰ δὲ τὰς αὐξήσεις ἀφαιρεῖν μέν τι αὐτῆς, προστιθέναι δὲ τῆς διαφορητικῆς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διαθέσεως

It is suitable, as I said, also for podagra and arthritis at every time 381 at which there are no violent pains requiring affusions and soothing cataplasms. When, then, such conditions (διαθέσεως) are beginning and still increasing, once you have melted the drug, having let it cool and scraping it with a spatula-probe, throw what has been scraped into a mortar, pouring in wine and rubbing it with the hands against the mortar, so that it takes up the wine. Let this wine be from the more austere ones, middling in age, not thick in consistency, but as far as possible rather transparent. For such wine, being of finer parts, sinks into the depth of the bodies. Pour it onto the melted drug up to this point, rubbing with the hands, until it takes it up and it is mixed in, and the liquid does not float alongside unmixed with the drug that is being rubbed and softened. And even without cooling it beforehand and scraping it, one will be able to mix in the wine by pouring it into the melted drug while it is still hot. At the beginnings of inflammations, then, the repellent power ought to prevail, but during the increases one should take away something of it and add some of the discutient power.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαθέσεως, σπαθομήλῃ, παραπλέῃ

It is suitable, as I said, both for podagra and for arthritis at every stage 381 in which there are no violent pains requiring soothing affusions and poultices. So while such conditions (διαθέσεως, apparently for διαθέσεων) are beginning and still increasing, once you have melted the drug, let it cool. Then scrape it with a spatula-probe, put the scrapings into a mortar, pour in wine, and work it with the hands at the mortar so that it takes up the wine. Let this wine be one of the more austere kinds, of middling age, not thick in consistency but as translucent as possible. For such a wine, being finer in its parts, penetrates into the depth of bodies. Pour it onto the melted drug only so far, working it with the hands, as it is taken up and mixed in, and the liquid does not float alongside, unmixed with the drug that is being worked and softened. One can also mix in the wine without first cooling and scraping, by pouring it into the melted drug while it is still warm. At the beginnings of inflammations, then, the repellent faculty ought to predominate. In the stages of increase one should take away something of it and add something of the discutient faculty.

ὅταν δ’ εἰς τὴν οἰκείαν ἀκμὴν ﻿ 382 ὁ τῆς φλεγμονῆς ὄγκος ἀφίκηται, παραπλησίας ἀλ λήλαις εἶναι χρὴ τὰς δυνάμεις τήν τε ἀποκρουστικὴν καὶ διαφορητικὴν, ἐάν γε μή τις ὀδύνη σφοδρὰ παρηγορικοῦ δέηται φαρμάκου. παρακμαζούσης δὲ τῆς φλεγμονῆς τὴν διαφορητικὴν δύναμιν ἐπικρατεῖν προσήκει, ὡς μηδ’ ὅλως ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ καιρῷ μίγνυσθαί τι τοῦ οἴνου. καὶ μέντοι καὶ κατ’ αὐτὴν τὴν τῆξιν, ἔνθα μὲν ἐπικρατεῖν ἱκανῶς βουλόμεθα τὴν στύφουσαν ποιότητα, τὸ Σπανὸν ἔλαιον ἢ τὸ Ἰστρικὸν ἢ τὸ καλούμενον ὀμφάκινόν τε καὶ ὠμοτριβὲς ἐμβάλλειν προσήκει. ἔνθα δὲ παραπλησίας ἔχειν ἀμφοτέρας τὰς δυνάμεις, ἐλαίῳ γλυκεῖ μήτε προσφάτῳ μήτε παλαιῷ τήκειν αὐτό. διαφορητικὸν δὲ ποιῆσαι βουλόμενοι, ἐπιτήδειόν ἐστι παλαιὸν ἔλαιον ἢ κίκινον. ἡ δὲ ἐν τῷ τήκεσθαι συμμετρία τοῦ ἐμπλαστοῦ φαρμάκου τοιάδε γιγνέσθω. βραχὺ πλέον ἔστω σοι τὸ ἔλαιον τοῦ φαρμάκου καὶ μᾶλλον δηλονότι τοῦ θέρους, ὡς εἶναι δέκα μὲν οὐγγίας τοῦ φαρμάκου, τοῦ δὲ ἐλαίου λίτραν μίαν αὐτάρκως ἂν ἐμβάλλοι τις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But when the swelling of the inflammation 382 has arrived at its proper peak, the faculties—the repellent and the discutient—must be comparable to one another, unless indeed some violent pain requires a soothing drug. And as the inflammation declines, the discutient faculty should prevail, so that at that time no wine whatsoever is mixed in. And moreover, also in the melting itself, where we wish the astringent quality to prevail considerably, it is fitting to put in Spanish oil, or Istrian oil, or that called omphakinon and omotribes (oil of unripe olives). But where we wish both faculties to be comparable, to melt it with sweet oil that is neither fresh nor old. And when we wish to make it discutient, old oil or castor oil (kikinon) is suitable. And let the proportion of the emplastic drug in the melting be as follows: let the oil be for you a little more than the drug, and more, obviously, in summer, so that there are ten ounces of the drug, while of the oil one would sufficiently put in one pound (litra).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀμφάκινόν, ὠμοτριβὲς

When the swelling of the inflammation has reached its proper peak 382, the powers, both the repellent and the discutient, must be similar to one another, unless some violent pain requires a soothing drug. But when the inflammation is declining, the discutient power should predominate, so that at that time none of the wine at all is mixed in. Moreover, in the melting itself as well, where we want the astringent quality to predominate sufficiently, one should put in Spanish oil or Istrian oil or the oil called omphacine and omotribes. Where we want it to have both powers similar, melt it with sweet oil that is neither fresh nor old. When we wish to make it discutient, old oil or castor oil is suitable. Let the proportion of the plaster drug in the melting be as follows. Let the oil be a little more than the drug, and evidently more so in summer, so that, the drug being ten ounces, one would add one pound (litra) of oil, and that would be sufficient.

εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι δραστικώτερον μὲν γίνεται πλέον ἔχον τοῦ φαρμάκου, παρηγορικώτερον 383 δὲ καὶ ἀνωδυνώτερον, ὅσῳ περ ἂν ὑγρότερον γένηται τακὲν σὺν ῥοδίνῳ. κηρωτῆς δὲ ἐγχρίστου σύστασιν λαμβάνει, τακείσης τοῦ φαρμάκου λίτρας μιᾶς ἐν ἡμιλίτρῳ ἐλαίου. χρήσιμον δὲ τὸ τοιοῦτο ταῖς παρακμαῖς τῶν φλεγμονῶν ἐστι. καὶ τοὺς ἐν ἕδρα δὲ καὶ κατὰ τὸ ἀπευθυσμένον ἔντερον ὄγκους φλεγμονώδεις αὐτομάτως γιγνομένους, οὐδὲν οὕτως ὀνίνησι. τετῆχθαι δὲ αὐτὸ χρὴ τηνικαῦτα μετ’ ἐλαίου πλείονος, ὡς δι’ εὐθυτρήτου κέρατος ἐνίεσθαι, κύστιν ὑείαν ἔχοντος προσδεδεμένην, ὡς εἰώθαμεν ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων χρῆσθαι. καὶ διὰ ῥοδίνου δὲ ἐπὶ τούτων ἔτηξα πολλάκις αὐτὸ, προϋποκειμένης τῷ ἀγγείῳ καθ’ ὃ τήκεται κακκάβης, ὕδωρ μὲν ἐν αὐτῇ ἐχούσης θερμὸν, ἄνθραξι δὲ διαπύροις ἐπικειμένης ἢ κατὰ φλογὸς ἀκάπνου. κατὰ μέντοι τὴν προειρημένην συμμετρίαν τετηκὸς, ὡς σύστασιν ἔχειν ὑγρᾶς κηρωτῆς καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὸ αἰδοῖον ἕλκη καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὴν ἕδραν ὀνίνησιν. οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ καὶ τἄλλα πάντα καὶ μέχρι τῶν ἑλκωθέντων χειμέθλων τε καὶ πυρικαύτων. καὶ γὰρ ἀφλέγμαντα καὶ καθαρὰ ποιεῖ ταχέως ἅπαντα καὶ σαρκοῖ στερεᾷ σαρκὶ καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἔχῃ πλέον χαλκίτεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐγχρίστου, εὐθυτρήτου

It is quite evident that it becomes more active when it contains more of the drug, but more soothing 383 and more pain-relieving the more liquid it becomes when melted with rose-oil. It takes on the consistency of a cerate for anointing when one litra of the drug is melted in half a litra of oil. Such a preparation is useful in the declines of inflammations. And nothing so benefits the inflammatory swellings that arise spontaneously in the seat and in the rectum. At that time it must have been melted with a greater quantity of oil, so that it can be injected through a straight-bored horn having a pig's bladder fastened to it, as we are accustomed to use in such cases. And in these cases I have often melted it with rose-oil, with a cauldron placed beneath the vessel in which it is melted, the cauldron containing hot water and set upon glowing coals or over a smokeless flame. When, however, it has been melted in the aforesaid proportion, so as to have the consistency of a liquid cerate, it benefits also the ulcers on the genitals and those on the seat, and not least all the others too, even down to ulcerated chilblains and burns. For it quickly renders them all free of inflammation and clean, and it fills them with flesh, and firm flesh at that, especially when it contains more chalcitis.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρόσφατον (object implied, presumably χυλόν), προεπιβρέξαντες

It is quite evident 384 that wine too must be mixed into the drug that is to accomplish this. And if it takes in addition some vinegar, it is best for burns, applied both at once and afterwards at every stage, whether the burn already has blisters or not. And it is obvious that even if someone is scalded by boiling water, this affection too is called a burn (pyrikauton). But if someone applies the drug before inflammation has set in, nothing so prevents the formation of inflammation, so that, as I said, some of those who operate on hernias use it immediately, without even having first moistened the wound. And for contusions and fractures and parts struck in any manner whatever it is most suitable when applied in liquid form. And when it is held in place with bandages in the manner of a fracture bandaging, it is of wonderful benefit to all oedemas and emphysemata (windy swellings). But when the body is full of bad humours or also excessively sensitive, one must, when applying it at the beginning to the bruised and struck parts, mix with it fresh [juice] of henbane.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ δ’ ἀποροίης προσφάτου, διὰ τὴν ὥραν τοῦ ἔτους 385 ἢ τὸ χωρίον οὐδὲν μεῖον ἕξεις ἐπεμβάλλων τὸν ἀποτεθέντα διὰ τοῦ θέρους, οἷος καὶ ὁ ἀπὸ Κρήτης ἐστὶ κομιζόμενος ἡμῖν εἰς Ῥώμην καθ’ ἕκαστον ἔτος. ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον τούτου τὸν χυλὸν τοῦ μανδραγόρου μιγνύων, ἀνώδυνον ἐργάσῃ τὸ φάρμακον. ἴστε δ’ ὅτι καὶ οὗτος ἀπὸ Κρήτης κομίζεται κάλλιστος ἐν ὀστρακίνοις ποτηρίοις. ἀρκεῖ δὲ μίαν οὐγγίαν μῖξαι τοῦ χυλοῦ, λίτρᾳ ἐλαίου τῆς τηκομένης ἐμπλάστρου. καὶ πλεῖον δ’ ἂν ἔσθ’ ὅτε καὶ ἔλαττον ἐμβάλλοις κατὰ τὴν χρείαν τῆς διαθέσεως. συντελεῖ δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων σωμάτων καὶ τὸ διὰ ῥοδίνου τετῆχθαι τὸ φάρμακον. ὅσα δὲ ἐκ καταπτώσεως ἐθλάσθη μέρη τοῦ σώματος ἢ πληγέντα ξύλῳ καὶ λίθῳ, κᾂν ἐκχύμωμά τι γένηται, κατὰ τὰς ἀρχὰς εὐθέως ἐπιτιθέναι χρὴ τὸ τετηκὸς φάρμακον, ὡς εἴρηται, μιχθέντος οἴνου. προσήκει δὲ κατὰ τὴν τρίτην ἡμέραν ἢ πάντως γε τὴν τετάρτην, ἐὰν ἀφλέγμαντον εἴη τὸ μόριον, ἀφαιρεῖν μὲν τὸν οἶνον, ἐλαίῳ δὲ τήκειν παλαιῷ τὴν ἔμπλαστρον. ἐὰν γὰρ ἐπὶ πλέον στύφηται τὰ ἐκχυμώματα δυσδιαφόρητα γίνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λίτρᾳ ἐλαίου τῆς τηκομένης ἐμπλάστρου

But if you should be at a loss for fresh (juice), on account of the season of the year 385 or the locality, you will have no less (benefit) by adding in that which was put away during the summer, such as is the kind brought to us at Rome from Crete every year. And still more than this, by mixing in the juice of mandrake you will make the drug anodyne. Know that this too is brought from Crete, of the finest quality, in earthenware cups. It suffices to mix one ounce of the juice to a pound of oil of the plaster that is being melted; and at times you may put in more, and at times less, according to the need of the condition. In the case of such bodies it also contributes that the drug be melted in rose-oil. As for those parts of the body that have been bruised from a fall, or struck with wood or stone, even if some ecchymoma arises, one must at the very beginning immediately apply the melted drug, as has been said, with wine mixed in. But it is fitting on the third day, or at all events the fourth, if the part should be free of inflammation, to remove the wine and to melt the plaster with old oil. For if the ecchymomata are astringed further, they become difficult to disperse.

If you lack fresh [henbane], on account of the season of the year 385 or the locality, you will have no less [benefit] by adding instead that which has been stored through the summer, such as is also brought to us to Rome from Crete every year. For if the ecchymoses are astringed further, they become hard to disperse.

Greek (Kühn)

τούτῳ τῷ φαρμάκῳ καὶ πρὸς ἐρυσιπέλατα χρῶμαι καὶ μάλιστα 386 φλεγμονώδη καὶ πολὺ δὴ μᾶλλον ἔτι πρὸς τὰς ἐρυσιπελατώδεις φλεγμονάς. ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν οὖν ἐπιτίθημι τήκων αὐτὸ ῥοδίνῳ δι’ ὠμοτριβοῦς ἐλαίου καὶ ἀνάλου σκευασθέντι, κάλλιστον μὲν γὰρ πρὸς ἐρυσιπέλατα τὸ τοιοῦτον. τακέντι δ’ οὕτως αὐτῷ μιγνύσθω κατὰ τὴν θυείαν, ἀναφυρόμενός τε καὶ δευόμενος ἀκριβῶς ὅλος δι’ ὅλου στρυχνοῦ χυλὸς ἢ εἰ μὴ τοῦτο παρείη ψυλλίου. θέρους δὲ ὄντος καὶ ὁ τῆς ὄμφακος μετὰ τοῦ τῆς ἀνδράχνης ἤ ἀειζώου. μόνος μὲν γὰρ ὁ τῆς ὄμφακος πλέον ἢ δεῖ στύφει. βλάπτει δὲ ἡ τοσαύτη στύψις ἐρυσιπέλατα, κατακλείει γὰρ αὐτῶν τὴν θερμότητα πυκνοῦσα τὸ δέρμα. πάλιν δὲ ὁ τῆς ἀνδράχνης χυλὸς, οὐδ’ ἐκθλιφθῆναι δύναται μόνος ὑπὸ γλισχρότητος. ἄριστον οὖν ἐστιν ἐκθλίψαντα τὸν τῆς ὄμφακος χυλὸν ἐπιβάλλειν ἐν ὅλμῳ κεκομμένης τῇ ἀνδράχνῃ καὶ οὕτως αὖθις κόπτειν. εἶθ’ ὅταν ἀκριβῶς ἑνωθῇ τὸ μικτὸν ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἐκθλίβειν τὸν χυλόν. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὸν τοῦ ἀειζώου χυλὸν ἐκθλίψεις. καταμόνας γὰρ οὐδὲ ἐκ τούτου δυνατὸν ἐκθλῖψαι δαψιλῆ, καθάπερ οὐδ’ ἐκ τῆς κοτυληδόνος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κεκομμένης

I use this drug also for erysipelata, and especially 386 those of a phlegmonous character, and still much more for erysipelatous phlegmonai. At the beginning, then, I apply it after melting it in rose-oil prepared from oil of unripe olives (omotribes) and unsalted; for such oil is best for erysipelata. And when it has been thus melted, let there be mixed with it in the mortar, kneaded in and thoroughly soaked through whole and entire, juice of strychnos, or, if this is not available, of psyllion. And when it is summer, also the juice of unripe grape (omphax) together with that of purslane or houseleek. For the juice of unripe grape by itself astringes more than is needed; and astringency of such a degree harms erysipelata, for it shuts in their heat by condensing the skin. Conversely, the juice of purslane cannot even be pressed out by itself because of its viscosity. It is best, therefore, after pressing out the juice of unripe grape, to pour it upon the purslane that has been pounded in a mortar, and so to pound it again; then, when the mixture of both has been thoroughly unified, to press out the juice. In the same way you will also press out the juice of houseleek; for on its own it is not possible to press out an abundant quantity from this either, just as not from kotyledon.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δευόμενος, ἀναφυρόμενος, στρυχνοῦ, κοτυληδόνος, ὅλμῳ

This drug I also use for erysipelases, and especially 386 for phlegmonous ones, and still much more for erysipelatous inflammations. At the beginning, then, I apply it melted in rose oil prepared from omotribes oil (oil of unripe olives) without salt; for such an oil is the finest for erysipelases. With it, once melted in this way, let there be mixed in the mortar the juice of strychnos (nightshade), being kneaded in and moistened precisely, wholly through the whole; or, if this is not at hand, that of psyllion (fleawort). When it is summer, also the juice of the unripe grape together with that of purslane or of aeizoon (houseleek). For the juice of the unripe grape on its own is more astringent than it should be, and so great an astringency harms erysipelases, since by condensing the skin it shuts in their heat. Purslane juice, on the other hand, cannot even be pressed out on its own because of its viscosity. Best, therefore, is to press out the juice of the unripe grape and pour it onto purslane that has been pounded in a pounding-mortar, and so pound it again; then, when the mixture of both has been precisely united, press out the juice. In the same way you will also press out the juice of the aeizoon; for neither from this is it possible to press out an abundant quantity on its own, just as it is not from kotyledon (navelwort) either.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And if you also press out fresh juice from the apples of the mandrake 387 when they are at their peak, you will make the drug more suitable. But if it should not be the season, one must at any rate mix in some of that which is brought from Crete or from some other such region. I am also accustomed to mix henbane juice with it, and sometimes mekoneion as well; this is the name given to the sap of the poppy. It is better, together with this, also to add in some glaukion. Much weaker than these, but easily procured, is the juice of thridakine (lettuce), both that of the wild kind and that of the cultivated, which all the Greeks of today call not thridakine but thridax, naming only the wild one thridakine. Weaker than these for cooling are the juices of seris (endive), of polygonon (knotgrass), of triphyllon (trefoil) and of mouse-ear; nevertheless these too cool, and more than they does the duckweed (phakos) from the marshes. If none of these is at hand, mix in vinegar and water. And all such drugs should have their acquired qualities cold, the vessels being set in very cold water or in snow.

And if you also press out fresh juice from the apples of the mandrake 387 when they are ripe, you will make the drug more suitable. I am also accustomed to mix in juice of henbane, and sometimes poppy juice (mēkōneion) as well; for this is the name given to the sap of the poppy. It is better also to add some glaucium together with this. Weaker than these for cooling are the juices of seris, of knotgrass, of trefoil and of mouse-ear; still, these too do cool. And more than these does the lentil that grows on stagnant pools. If none of these is to hand, mix in vinegar and water. Let all such drugs have their acquired qualities cold, the vessels being set in very cold water or in snow.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And let the phoinikine drug itself likewise be cooled when it is applied 388 to erysipelata, until their very fiery character has ceased. It is quite evident that the things which benefit erysipelatous conditions through an acquired cold quality must be changed continually, and especially when the erysipelas is exact, without being mixed with phlegmone. When, however, the very fiery character has ceased, or when from the outset there was no exact erysipelas at all, but either an erysipelatous phlegmone or a phlegmonous erysipelas, we no longer have any need of the acquired cooling; rather, we shall also abstain from the strongly cooling juices, mixing the moderately cold ones with the melted drug. And after these let us now also mix in juice of linseed and of chamomile; and after this we shall use the melted drug by itself alone, mixing in nothing else.

And let the drug made with the palm plaster (phoinikinē) itself likewise be thoroughly chilled when it is applied 388 to cases of erysipelas, until their intensely fiery character ceases. It is quite clear that the remedies which benefit erysipelatous conditions through an acquired cold quality must be changed continually, and especially when the erysipelas is pure, without any admixture of inflammation (phlegmonē). But if at some point the erysipelas has already become livid, from the power of the cooling agents, one ought no longer even to call such a thing erysipelas; and at that time it is fitting to abstain not only from the admixture of wine, but also from rose-oil and the astringent oils, for phoinikine alone, melted in old oil, is sufficient to disperse what has become livid 389, and at that time the affected parts are fomented with hot water and scarified. And if it should ever seem good to you to do this, one must at once apply the liquid drug of the melted phoinikine containing wine, and after one day remove the wine. But if we take over an erysipelas already cooled to a greater degree by physicians who have cooled it immoderately, so that it is already even turning black, we shall make greater use of the fomentation and the scarification. And apart from these, we shall mix some unslaked lime into the liquid drug; but if the affected parts are soft-fleshed, we shall mix in washed lime. Good at that time also is the juice of coriander mixed into the drug. This indeed, even with rose cerate, is of great benefit; and for these, namely such conditions, many other drugs as well will be described in the following discussion, some having a power similar to phoinikine, others even more active.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσπεπτωκότα, ἐν πλέονι χρόνῳ τούτου συμβάντος

For rheumatic conditions this is the best drug of all, and one that has often accomplished something beyond hope. 390 Thus once, when a thigh had suppurated near the groin, the inflammation that occupied the whole of it being unconcocted, the leg was positioned by us so that the ichors would flow out toward the groin. Through the ulcer that formed—this having happened over a rather long time—the flux that had settled into the thigh was indeed evacuated, but a certain fistulous condition arose beneath the skin throughout the whole thigh, so that I wished to divide it near the kneecap, in order that on either side there might be an outlet for the ichors, and an easy passage for the liquid drugs that were injected to pass through further. But when the thigh was unbound on the following day, on which I had resolved to cut it, everything was found to have fallen together, dry and without moisture. It is clear, then, that on that day too it was reasonable to use the drug, and likewise on the next, and on the following days, since on each of them the part became better-coloured and as a whole healthier.

For fluxional conditions this is the best drug of all, and it has often accomplished something greater than was hoped for. 390 Thus, on one occasion, when a thigh had suppurated near the groin while the inflammation throughout the whole of it was unconcocted, the leg was positioned by us so that the ichors would flow out towards the groin. It was plain, then, that it was reasonable to use the drug both on that day and likewise on the next, and on the days following, since on each of them the part became of better colour and, as a whole, healthier.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

So, when the youth had been cured in this way, I afterwards treated many rheumatic conditions with the drug, ones which had a single opening, 391 but whose interior was unagglutinated and fistulous. One must not desist from its use, even if it appears to bring no perceptible benefit for many days. For later, at some point, in a single day it displays a very great benefit all at once; so use it with confidence always in rheumatic conditions, when a soft-fleshed person or some child or woman is affected, adding, in accordance with the aforesaid proportion, four ounces of chalcitis; but when the affected body is strong and hard, adding in proportion either one ounce or two, so that there are five or six ounces of chalcitis, three pounds of each of the two, the old oil and the litharge, and two of the old fat, which I said those who sell it call axoungion. And in rheumatic conditions let it be diluted with sweet oil that is neither fresh nor old, and let it take in addition the austere wine, this being of middle age.

So, the youth having been treated in this way, afterwards I treated with the drug many fluxional conditions which had a single opening, 391 but whose interior was unagglutinated and fistulous. One must not give up using it, even if it appears to give no perceptible benefit for many days. For later, at some point, within a single day it displays a very great benefit all at once. So use it with confidence throughout in fluxional conditions: when the sufferer is a soft-fleshed person, or some child or woman, putting in 4 ounces of the chalcitis in accordance with the proportion stated before. But when the affected body is strong and hard, add proportionately either one ounce or two, so that there are 5 or 6 ounces of the chalcitis, three litrae of each, both of the old oil and of the litharge, and two of the old fat, which, as I said, the sellers call axungia. In fluxional conditions let it be thinned with sweet oil, neither fresh nor old, and let it take in addition the austere wine, being of middle age.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐδὲ γὰρ ἕξει τὸ παρηγορικὸν οὔτε ἐκ μόνων τῶν χαλώντων τὸ διαφορητικὸν ἄνευ στύψεως γενόμενον

This drug has refuted the nonsense of Thessalus, since it displays a most effective benefit while being composed of ingredients opposite in their faculties, of which, if you remove 392 the one, it will accomplish none of the things mentioned. For neither, from the astringents alone without the relaxants, will it accomplish anything noteworthy in rheumatic conditions, or in general in inflammations that are beginning and increasing—for it will not have the soothing quality—nor, from the relaxants alone, will it have the discutient quality, when it is made without astringency, relaxants to which it belongs to soften what is becoming hard. It would, however, be thoroughly relaxant and moderately emollient, if it had the oil and the fat not very old. At any rate the drug which Mnaseas compounded has the oil, the litharge and the fat in equal amounts to one another, and it is most contrary to inflammations that are beginning and increasing. Clearly this drug of ours, too, has all the things that Mnaseas' drug has: the litharge, the fat and the oil. But if it takes in none of the astringents—?

This drug exposed the nonsense of Thessalus: it displays a most efficacious benefit, yet it is compounded of ingredients opposite in their powers, and if you take away 392 one of the two, it will accomplish none of the things described. For from the astringent ingredients alone, without the relaxing ones, it will achieve nothing excellent in fluxional conditions, or generally in inflammations that are beginning and increasing. For it will not have the soothing quality. Nor, from the relaxing ingredients alone, will it have the discutient quality, which arises without astringency; to these relaxing ingredients belongs the power of softening what has become hardened. It would be precisely relaxant and moderately emollient if it did not contain the oil and the fat very old. At any rate, the drug that Mnasaeus compounded contains the oil, the litharge and the fat in equal amounts to one another, and it is most contrary to inflammations that are beginning and increasing. Clearly this drug of ours also contains the same ingredients as that of Mnasaeus: the litharge, the fat and the oil. But what if it takes up none of the astringent ingredients?

it makes the parts effeminate and dissolves their tone, for which reason they also become rheumatic; for you have learned that it is not always through much heat, as some suppose, that the parts of the body are afflicted with flux, but this happens to them 393 rarely.
