But it is no wonder that Thessalus, being inexperienced in the works of the medical art and unable to discover the conditions by reasoning, is ignorant of everything that pertains to the cures of inflammations; but you, who have often observed the most evident benefit, in many conditions, both of this phoenicine plaster and of other drugs composed of opposite powers, must yourselves also attempt, whenever you are at a loss 394 for those already compounded, to compound drugs according to the same method, from the materials readily available in that locality—when an inflammation is expected to occur, or has even already begun, drugs having more astringency; during the increases themselves, less; and during the acmes and declines, drugs having more of the relaxing and discutient power. Always, however, let both be mixed, the repellent and the discutient, except if at some time, when the influxes have already completely ceased and the body is empty, one might without danger proceed to the discutient powers, just as in the first onsets to the repellent ones. [On the plasters prepared with litharge.] Since, in the composition of my phoenicine plaster, I said that the discutient power lies in the old fat and in the oil, and the contracting and repellent power in the chalcitis and in the date, and these cannot without litharge produce an emplastic drug, for this reason it seemed good to me to go through next the matter of the use of litharge.

But it is no wonder that Thessalus, being inexperienced in the works of the medical art and unable to discover the conditions by reason, is ignorant of everything concerning the cures of inflammations. You, however, who have often observed the most evident benefit, in many conditions, both of this phoinikinē and of other drugs compounded from opposite powers, must yourselves also attempt, whenever at some time you lack 394 those already compounded, to compound drugs according to the same method from what is readily available in that place. [On plasters prepared with litharge.] Since, in the composition of my phoinikinē plaster, I said that there is a discutient power in the old fat and the oil, and a contractive and repellent one in the chalcitis and the palm, and since these cannot make a plaster drug without the litharge, it therefore seemed good to me to discuss next the use of litharge.

εἰς μὲν γὰρ τὴν δύναμιν τῆς φοινικίνης 395 οὔτ’ ἀντιπράττειν τι νομιστέον αὐτὴν οὔτε συντελεῖν, ὕλης δὲ λόγον ἔχειν μόνον ἐπιτηδείου πρὸς ἐμπλαστοῦ φαρμάκου ποίησιν. εὔλογον οὖν ἔδοξέ μοι τὸν περὶ τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων λόγον ὡς ἐπὶ παραδείγματος τῆς λιθαργύρου διελθεῖν. ἔστι δὲ οὐκ ὀλίγα δύναμιν μὲν ἢ οὐδεμίαν ἢ πάνυ σμικρὰν τῷ σκευαζομένῳ συνεισφερόμενα, χρείαν δ’ ὕλης παρέχοντα πρὸς τὸ γενέσθαι φάρμακον ἐμπλαστὸν ἢ κηρωτῶδες. οὔτε γὰρ θερμαίνουσα σαφῶς ἡ λιθάργυρος, καθάπερ οὐδὲ ψύχουσα οὐδ’ ὑγραίνουσα βραχεῖαν ἔχει δύναμιν ξηραντικὴν κατὰ τὴν πρώτην ἀπόστασιν τῶν ἀκριβῶς μέσων, τοῦθ’ ὑγραίνειν καὶ ξηραίνειν· οὐ μὴν οὐδ’ ἐμπλαστικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ὥσπερ οὐδ’ ἐκφρακτικῆς, ὡσαύτως δὲ οὐδὲ ῥυπτικῆς, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ ῥυπαινούσης σαφῶς, ἠρέμα δέ πως ἀποκεχώρηκε τῶν μέσων ἐπὶ τὸ παχυμερέστερον. ἀλλὰ κατὰ τὴν ἐπὶ πλέον ἕψησιν ἀποτίθεται καὶ τοῦτο, χεομένη τε καὶ ἀλλοιουμένη διὰ τῆς τοῦ πυρὸς δυνάμεως. μόνη δὲ καθ’ ἑαυτὴν ὠμὴ παρατρίμματα μηρῶν ἐξ ὁδοιπορίας ὀνίνησιν, ᾧ καὶ δῆλον ὅτι βραχέως ξηραίνει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῦθ’ ὑγραίνειν καὶ ξηραίνειν, ἐμπλαστικῆς

For with respect to the power of the phoinikine plaster 395 one must suppose that it neither counteracts it in any way nor contributes to it, but has only the status of a material suitable for the making of an emplastic drug. It therefore seemed to me reasonable to go through the account of such drugs with litharge as an example. There are not a few of them that contribute either no power or a very small one to what is being prepared, but which supply the service of a material toward the drug's becoming emplastic or cerate-like. For litharge, neither clearly heating, just as it is neither cooling nor moistening, has a slight drying power, at the first remove from the things that are exactly intermediate, in this respect, namely moistening and drying; nor indeed is it of a pore-blocking power, just as it is not of a de-obstructing one, and likewise it is neither detergent, just as it is not clearly befouling, but it has departed somewhat gently from the intermediates toward the thicker-in-parts. But in the course of more prolonged boiling it lays aside even this, being melted and altered by the power of the fire. Alone and by itself, raw, it benefits chafings of the thighs arising from walking, from which it is also clear that it dries slightly.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοῦθ’ ὑγραίνειν καὶ ξηραίνειν, ἀποτίθεται καὶ τοῦτο

For as regards the power of the phoenicine plaster 395 one must consider that it neither counteracts anything nor contributes anything; it has only the status of a material suitable for the making of a plaster drug. It therefore seemed reasonable to me to go through the account of such drugs using litharge as an example. There are not a few substances that contribute either no power at all or a very small one to the preparation, but furnish the service of material for the production of a plaster or cerate-like drug. For litharge does not perceptibly warm, just as it does not cool or moisten either. It has a slight drying power at the first remove from the exact mean, that is, from the mean between moistening and drying. Nor indeed is it of an emplastic power, just as it is not of a deobstruent one. Likewise it is not detergent, just as it is not perceptibly soiling either. It has, however, departed somewhat gently from the mean toward the thicker-particled. But in more prolonged boiling it lays aside even this, as it melts and is altered through the power of the fire. Alone by itself and raw, it benefits chafings of the thighs from walking journeys, from which it is also clear that it dries slightly.

τοῦ δὲ 396 μήτε δριμεῖαν ἔχειν αὐτὴν ἢ γλυκεῖαν ποιότητα μήτ’ ὀξεῖαν ἢ πικρὰν ἢ αὐστηρὰν ἢ στρυφνὴν ἢ ὅλως στυπτικὴν, ἡ γεῦσις ἱκανὴ μάρτυς, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ ἕλκη, μήτε δακνόμενα πρὸς αὐτῆς μήτε θερμαινόμενα μήτε ψυχόμενα μήτε καθαιρούμενα μήτε σαρκούμενα μήθ’ ὅταν ὑπερσαρκώσῃ καθαιρούμενα. πάρεστιν οὖν ἡμῖν εἰς πολλὰ κεχρῆσθαι λιθαργύρῳ, ποτὲ μὲν ἐλαίῳ καὶ ὕδατι τὴν ἕψησιν αὐτῆς ποιουμένοις ἄχρι συστάσεως ἐμπλαστρώδους, ἔστι δ’ ὅτε ἐν ἐλαίῳ καὶ ὄξει καί ποτε ἐν ἐλαίῳ καὶ οἴνῳ. ἐν οἷς δ’ ἂν ἕψηται τούτων, ἄμεινον γίγνεται τὸ φάρμακον ἐπὶ πλέον ἑψηθέν. ἔσται δὲ τοῦτο τῷ πλήθει τῶν μιγνυμένων ὑγρῶν. ἔστι μὲν γὰρ τῶν τηκτῶν φαρμάκων καὶ ἡ λιθάργυρος ὅλη χεομένη, καθάπερ ἥ τε ῥητίνη καὶ ὁ κηρὸς, ἀλλ’ οὐκ εὐθέως ἅμα τῷ ψαῦσαι τοῦ πυρὸς, ὅθεν καὶ λανθάνει πολλοὺς καὶ νομίζουσιν αὐτὴν οὐδ’ ὅλως χεῖσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And that it has 396 neither a pungent or sweet quality, nor a sour or bitter or harsh or astringent or, in general, styptic one, taste is a sufficient witness, as are also the ulcers, which are neither bitten by it nor heated nor cooled nor cleansed nor filled with flesh, nor, when they have grown excess flesh, reduced. It is therefore open to us to use litharge for many purposes, sometimes carrying out its boiling with oil and water until a plaster-like consistency is reached, sometimes in oil and vinegar, and sometimes in oil and wine. In whichever of these it is boiled, the drug becomes better when boiled for a longer time. And this will be achieved by the quantity of the liquids mixed in. For litharge too is among the meltable drugs, being wholly fused, just like resin and wax, but not immediately upon touching the fire; hence it escapes the notice of many, and they think that it is not fused at all.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στρυφνὴν, ὑπερσαρκώσῃ καθαιρούμενα, χεομένη

That 396 it has neither an acrid nor a sweet quality, nor a sour, bitter, austere, acerbic, or in general astringent one, the taste is a sufficient witness, as are also ulcers, which are neither bitten by it, nor heated, nor cooled, nor cleansed, nor filled with flesh, nor, when they have produced excess flesh, cleaned down. It is therefore open to us to use litharge for many purposes, sometimes carrying out its boiling with oil and water until it reaches a plaster-like consistency, at other times in oil and vinegar, and sometimes in oil and wine. K13.396-397K. XIII 396–397, ch. 1.5 difficulty low (2.73/100; thin 1, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

σὺν ὀξελαίῳ μὲν οὖν ἑψομένη τήκεται θᾶττον, ὥσπερ γε καὶ σὺν ἰῷ καὶ αὐτοῦ δηλονότι τοῦ ἰοῦ χεῖσθαι πεφυκότος, ὥσπερ ἥ τε χαλκῖτις ﻿ 397 καὶ τὸ μίσυ. τάχιστα μὲν οὖν αὐτῶν ἡ χαλκῖτις τοῦτο πάσχει, βραδύτατα δ’ ὁ λιθάργυρος· ἐν δὲ τῷ μεταξὺ τούτων ὅ τε ἰός ἐστι καὶ τὸ μίσυ. χεῖται καὶ τὸ σῶρυ χρόνῳ πλέονι, γεωδέστερόν τε γάρ ἐστι καὶ λιθωδέστερον τῆς χαλκίτεώς τε καὶ τοῦ μίσυος. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τούτων αὖθις εἰρήσεται. τὴν λιθάργυρον δὲ δι’ ὀξελαίου μὲν ἕψων θᾶττον τήξῃς, δι’ ὑδρελαίου δὲ βραδύτερον, ὅμως μὲν χεῖται καὶ οὕτως, ἐὰν ᾖ διπλάσιον αὐτῆς ἑκάτερον τῶν ὑγρῶν. ἐγὼ δὲ καὶ πλέον ἢ διπλάσιον ἔμιξα πολλάκις, ὡς ὀλίγον ὕστερον εἰρήσεται. κάλλιον δ’ ἐστὶν εὐθέως ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἐν θυείᾳ μιγνύειν τὰ τρία, τήν τε λιθάργυρον καὶ τὸ ὕδωρ καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον. εἰ δὲ τοῦ λευκὸν γίνεσθαι τὸ φάρμακον φροντίζεις, ἔλαιόν τε καθαρώτερον καὶ διαυγέστερον ἔμβαλλε καὶ ὕδωρ ὁμοίως καθαρόν τε καὶ λευκὸν, ἑψέσθω τε κατὰ χωρίον ὑπαίθριον ἡλιούμενον. ὁ μὲν γὰρ ζοφώδης ἀὴρ μελαίνει τὸ φάρμακον, ὁ δὲ καθαρὸς εἰς λευκότητα μεγάλως συντελεῖ, καθάπερ γε καὶ τὸ καταῤῥαινόμενον ὕδωρ ἐν ἀέρι τοιούτῳ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καταῤῥαινόμενον

When boiled with vinegar-and-oil (oxelaion), then, it melts more quickly, just as it also does with verdigris (ios), verdigris itself obviously being of a nature to be fused, as are chalcitis 397 and misy. Of these, chalcitis undergoes this most quickly, litharge most slowly; and in between these are verdigris and misy. Sory too is fused, in a longer time, for it is more earthy and more stony than chalcitis and misy. But about these things it will be spoken again later. As for litharge, if you boil it in vinegar-and-oil you will melt it more quickly, in water-and-oil (hydrelaion) more slowly; nevertheless it is fused even so, provided that each of the liquids is double its quantity. I myself have often mixed in even more than double, as will be said a little later. It is better to mix the three together straightaway from the start in a mortar, the litharge, the water and the oil. And if you are concerned that the drug should come out white, put in oil that is purer and more transparent, and water likewise pure and clear, and let it be boiled in an open-air spot exposed to the sun. For murky air blackens the drug, whereas clear air contributes greatly to whiteness, just as does the water sprinkled over it in such air.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὥσπερ γε καὶ σὺν ἰῷ, καταῤῥαινόμενον

When boiled with oxelaeum (vinegar and oil), then, it melts more quickly, just as it does also with verdigris, since verdigris itself is evidently of a nature to liquefy, as are chalcitis 397 and misy. Of these, chalcitis undergoes this most quickly and litharge most slowly; verdigris and misy lie between them. Sory also liquefies, but over a longer time, for it is more earthy and more stony than chalcitis and misy. But these things will be discussed again later. Litharge you will melt more quickly if you boil it with oxelaeum, and more slowly with hydrelaeum (water and oil); yet it liquefies in this way too, if each of the two liquids is double its amount. I myself have often mixed in even more than double, as will be said a little later. It is better to mix the three together in a mortar right from the beginning: the litharge, the water and the oil. If you are concerned that the drug should turn out white, put in oil that is purer and more translucent, and water that is likewise pure and white, and let it be boiled in an open-air place exposed to the sun. For murky air blackens the drug, whereas clear air contributes greatly to its whiteness, as does also the water sprinkled onto it in such air.

Greek (Kühn)

φυλάττου δὲ καὶ τὸ διὰ ξύλων ποιεῖσθαι τὴν ἕψησιν ἢ ἀνθράκων ἀρχομένων διακάεσθαι, 398 μελαίνει γὰρ αὐτὸ ῥᾳδίως ὁ καπνός. προδιακαύσας οὖν τοὺς ἄνθρακας, ὅταν ἀκριβῶς ὦσι διάπυροι, τότε ἕψειν ἄρξαι. καὶ εἰ μὴ φθάσοιεν οὗτοι συμπληρῶσαι τὴν ἕψησιν, ἐφ’ ἑτέρας ἑστίας ὁμοίως παρασκευάσας ἄνθρακας ἐπιτίθει κατ’ ἐκείνην τὴν κακκάβην. ἕνεκα μὲν οὖν τοῦ λευκὸν γενέσθαι τὸ φάρμακον, ὅσα χρὴ παραφυλάττειν αὐτάρκως εἴρηται. γίγνεται δὲ ἐχεκολλότερον εἰς τοσοῦτον, εἰς ὅσον ἂν ἔχῃ πλέον, ὡς εἴρηται, τὸ ὑγρόν. ὡς ἐάν γε τῶν τριῶν ἀνὰ λίτραν α΄. ἐμβάλῃς, δυσαπόπτωτον ἔσται. καὶ μέντοι καὶ ἄτηκτος ἡ λιθάργυρος μένει, καὶ τοῦτό γέ ἐστιν αὐτὸ μάλιστα αἴτιον τοῦ ῥᾳδίως ἀποπίπτειν αὐτὸ, καθάπερ γε καὶ τὸ προσμένειν μέχρι πλέονος, εἰ ἡ λιθάργυρος ἑψηθεῖσα χυθείη τε καὶ ἀλλοιωθείη. λευκότερον δ’ ἔσται καὶ θᾶττον ἑψηθήσεται βληθεισῶν δύο λιτρῶν ὕδατος εἰς τρεῖς ἑκατέρου λιθαργύρου τε καὶ ἐλαίου. καὶ τούτῳ συνήθως ἐπὶ τῶν μικρῶν ἑλκῶν χρῶμαι. πρόδηλος δὲ ἡ δύναμίς ἐστι τοῦ δι’ ὕδατος ἑψομένου φαρμάκου μεμνημένοις ἡμῖν τῶν εἰρημένων ἐν τῇ περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείᾳ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δυσαπόπτωτον, κατ’ ἐκείνην τὴν κακκάβην

Beware also of carrying out the boiling over wood, or over charcoals that are just beginning to burn through, 398 for the smoke easily blackens it. So burn the charcoals through beforehand, and when they are thoroughly glowing, then begin to boil. And if these should not suffice to complete the boiling, prepare charcoals in the same way on another hearth and set them under that cauldron. As regards the drug becoming white, then, what one must guard against has been stated sufficiently. It becomes more adhesive to the degree that it contains more of the liquid, as has been said; so that if you put in one litra of each of the three, it will be hard to fall off (δυσαπόπτωτον). And indeed the litharge remains unmelted, and this very thing is the chief cause of its easily falling off, just as, conversely, of its staying on for longer, if the litharge, having been boiled, has been fused and altered. It will be whiter and will be boiled more quickly if two litrai of water are put in to three of each, of litharge and of oil. And this I customarily use for small ulcers. The power of the drug boiled with water is evident to us if we remember what was said in the treatise on simple drugs.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δυσαπόπτωτον (the sense expected from the argument, that with equal parts the plaster falls off easily, suggests a corruption, perhaps for εὐαπόπτωτον), ἐπιτίθει κατ’ ἐκείνην τὴν κακκάβην (exact placement of the fresh coals relative to the pot), ἐχεκολλότερον, ἀρχομένων διακάεσθαι

Guard also against carrying out the boiling over wood, or over coals that are only beginning to burn through, 398 for the smoke readily blackens it. Having therefore burnt the coals through beforehand, when they are thoroughly glowing, then begin to boil. And if these should not hold out long enough to complete the boiling, prepare coals in the same way on another hearth and set them in place under that pot. As regards the drug's becoming white, then, what must be watched for has been sufficiently stated. It becomes more adhesive to the degree that it has more liquid, as has been said. So that if you put in one litra of each of the three, it will be hard to detach. And moreover the litharge also remains unmelted, and this is itself the chief cause of its readily falling off, just as also of its staying on for longer, if the litharge on being boiled has been dissolved and altered. It will be whiter and will boil more quickly if two litrae of water are put in to three of each, of litharge and of oil. And I customarily use this on small wounds. The power of the drug boiled with water is evident to us if we remember what was said in the treatise on simple drugs.

Greek (Kühn)

399 [Περὶ τῆς διὰ λιθαργύρου καὶ ὑδρελαίου σκευαζομένης ἐμπλάστρου.] Τὸ ὕδωρ ὑγραίνει μὲν ἀεὶ καὶ ψύχει κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν οὐσίαν· ἐπίκτητον δὲ θερμότητα προσλαβὸν, ἄχρι περ ἂν αὐτὴν διαφυλάττῃ θερμαίνει, ψυχρὸν δὲ γενόμενον ψύχει. τό γε μὴν ἔλαιον, ἐὰν εἴη νέον, οὐκ ἀντιπράττει τῷ φαρμάκῳ, διαμένει γὰρ ὑγρόν τε καὶ μετρίως ψυχρὸν, ὁποίου μάλιστα δεῖται τά θ’ ἁπαλόσαρκα σώματα καὶ τὰ κακόχυμα. γυναῖκές τε οὖν καὶ παῖδες καὶ τῶν τελείων οἱ μαλακὴν ἔχοντες τὴν ἕξιν τοῦ σώματος, εἰσὶ δ’ οὗτοι καὶ λευκοὶ κατὰ τὸ χρῶμα, τῶν τοιούτων δέονται φαρμάκων. ἑψηθὲν δ’, ὡς εἴρηται, μετὰ πλείονος ἐλαίου τε καὶ ὕδατος οὕτως γίνεται δυσαπόπτωτον, ὡς μηδὲ λουομένων ἀποῤῥεῖν. καὶ μέντοι καὶ συμφέρει τοῖς ἐπουλουμένοις ἕλκεσιν ἐπιμένειν τὸ φάρμακον ἐφεξῆς ἡμερῶν πλεόνων, ἄμεινόν τε γὰρ οὕτω καὶ θᾶττον ἐπουλοῦται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

399 [On the plaster prepared from litharge and water-and-oil (hydrelaion).] Water always moistens and cools according to its own proper substance; but when it has taken on an acquired heat, it warms for as long as it retains that heat, and when it has become cold it cools. The oil, for its part, if it is fresh, does not work against the drug, for it remains both moist and moderately cold, which is precisely what soft-fleshed bodies and those full of bad humours most require. Women, then, and children, and those among adults who have a soft habit of body — and these are also white in colour — need drugs of this kind. Boiled, as has been said, with a larger quantity of oil and water, it becomes so hard to fall off that it does not even run off when they bathe. And indeed it is beneficial for wounds that are cicatrizing that the drug remain in place for several days in succession, for in this way they cicatrize both better and more quickly.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

399 [On the plaster prepared with litharge and hydrelaeum (water and oil).] Water always moistens and cools by its own proper substance; but when it has taken on an acquired heat, it heats for as long as it retains that heat, and when it has become cold, it cools. Oil, however, if it is fresh, does not work against the drug, for it remains moist and moderately cold, which is the kind of thing that soft-fleshed bodies and cacochymic bodies most need. Women, then, and children, and those among adults who have a soft habit of body (these are also white in colour), need drugs of this kind. Boiled, as has been said, with a larger quantity of oil and water, it becomes so hard to dislodge that it does not run off even when the patients bathe. Moreover, it is also beneficial for ulcers that are cicatrizing that the drug remain on for several days in succession, for in this way they cicatrize both better and more quickly.

K13.399-400K. XIII 399–400, ch. 1.6 difficulty low (1.89/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) fail (b) fail 4/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: fail

Greek (Kühn)

πρόδηλον δ’ ὅτι κνήσμασι καὶ ἀποσύρμασι καὶ συνελόντι φάναι μικροῖς ἕλκεσιν ἁρμόττει τὸ τοιοῦτον φάρμακον, ἐφ’ ὧν καὶ ἡ πάρυγρος ὀνομαζομένη κηρωτὴ καὶ ἄλλαι τινὲς ὅμοιαι χρησιμώτεραι 400 τῶν ἰσχυρὰν δύναμιν ἐχόντων εἰσὶν, ὁποῖα τοῖς μεγάλοις τραύμασιν ἐπιτίθεμεν. ὑπὲρ ὧν ἁπάντων τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γέγραπται, καθ’ ὃ τοὺς γενικοὺς καὶ κοινοὺς λόγους, οὓς καθόλου καλοῦσιν, ἐφ’ ἑκάστης διαφορᾶς ἕλκους, διῆλθον ἐπιδεικνὺς ὁποίας τινὰς εἶναι προσήκει τὰς δυνάμεις τῶν ἰασομένων αὐτὰ φαρμάκων, ἐνταυθοῖ δὲ τὰς κατὰ μέρος ὕλας διέρχομαι, συμμετρίας τε τῶν μιγνυμένων ὁρίζων, ἐπιδεικνύς τε τοὺς τρόπους ἐν οἷς σκευαζόμενα γενήσεται κάλλιστα. δι’ ὑδρελαίου μὲν οὖν ἡ λιθάργυρος, ὡς εἴρηται, σκευαζομένη τὴν μὲν ἔμπλαστρον εὐαφεστάτην τε ἅμα καὶ δυσαπόπτωτον ἐργάζεται, καὶ προσέτι λευκὴν ὀφθῆναι, δύναμιν ἔχουσαν ψυκτικὴν μετρίαν, ᾗ χρωμένη καὶ κωλύει τὶ τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μορίοις ἐπιῤῥεῖν καὶ τὸ φθάσαν ἐπιῤῥυῆναι, πάλιν ἀποκρούεται πρὸς τοὺς πέριξ τόπους, ὡς γίνεσθαι τὸ πρᾶγμα τοιοῦτον, οἷον ὅταν ὕδατι ψυχρῷ διάβροχον ἐργασάμενοι σπόγγον ἐπιθῶμέν τινι μορίῳ τοῦ σώματος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It is evident that such a drug is suited to scratches and abrasions and, in a word, to small ulcers, for which the cerate called "moist" (parhygros) and certain other similar ones are more useful 400 than those having a strong power, such as we apply to large wounds. Concerning the general power of all of these it has been written in the third book of the Therapeutic Method, in which I went through the generic and common accounts, which they call universal, for each difference of ulcer, showing of what sort the powers of the drugs that are to cure them ought to be; here, however, I go through the particular materials, both defining the proportions of the ingredients mixed and showing the manners in which, when prepared, they will turn out best. Litharge, then, prepared with water-and-oil (hydrelaion), as has been said, makes the plaster at once most agreeable to the touch and hard to fall off, and moreover white in appearance, having a moderate cooling power, by the use of which it both prevents anything from flowing to the ulcerated parts and drives back again toward the surrounding places what has already flowed in, so that the matter becomes such as when we make a sponge soaked in cold water and apply it to some part of the body.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εὐαφεστάτην, χρωμένη

It is obvious that such a drug suits scratches and abrasions and, to put it briefly, small ulcers; in these cases the cerate called 'moist' and certain other similar ones are more useful 400 than drugs having a strong power, of the kind we apply to large wounds. Concerning the general power of all of these I have written in the third book of the Therapeutic Method, in which I went through the generic and common accounts, which they call universal, for each differentia of ulcer, showing of what kind the powers of the drugs that are to heal them ought to be; here, however, I go through the particular materials, defining the proportions of the ingredients that are mixed, and showing the ways in which, when prepared, they will turn out best. Litharge, then, prepared with hydrelaeum (water and oil), as has been said, makes the plaster at once most pleasant to the touch and hard to fall off, and moreover white to look at, having a moderate cooling power. Employing this power, it both prevents anything from flowing into the ulcerated parts and drives back again toward the surrounding places what has already flowed in. The effect is thus like what happens when we make a sponge soaked through with cold water and apply it to some part of the body.

K13.400-401K. XIII 400–401, ch. 1.6, 1.7 difficulty mid (4.52/100; thin 4, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐκεῖνός τε γὰρ ῥυσὸν καὶ προσεσταλμένον ἐργάζεται τὸν τόπον ὅλον, οὐ 401 μόνον ἀποκρουόμενος τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν αὐτῷ πρὸς τοὺς πλησιάζοντας ἐκθλίβων. ἥ τε νῦν εἰρημένη κατὰ τὸν λόγον ἔμπλαστρος, οὐ κατὰ πρώτην δύναμιν, ἀλλὰ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ξηραίνειν ἂν λεχθείη. κυρίως μὲν γὰρ καὶ πρώτως τά τε διαφοροῦντα καὶ τὰ τὰς κράσεις ὑπαλλάττοντα πρὸς τὸ ξηρότερον ὀνομάζεται ξηραίνοντα. κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς δὲ καὶ τὰ διὰ ψύξιν, ἀναστέλλοντα μὲν τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον, ἐκθλίβοντα δὲ τὸ περιεχόμενον. ὅλως δὲ οὐδὲν τῶν ὑγραινόντων ἢ πρώτως ἢ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ὠφέλιμον ἕλκεσίν ἐστιν, ὡς ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ δέδεικται τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου. [Περὶ τῆς διὰ λιθαργύρου καὶ ὀξελαίου σκευαζομένης ἐμπλάστρου.] Περὶ μὲν οὖν τῆς δι’ ὑδρελαίου σκευαζομένης λιθαργύρου καὶ ταῦθ’ ἱκανά. μεταβῆναι δὲ ἤδη καιρὸς ἐπὶ τὴν δι’ ὀξελαίου συντιθεμένην. συμμέτρως μὲν οὖν ξηραντικὸν γίνεται τὸ φάρμακον, ἐὰν διπλάσιον ἑκάτερον ᾖ τῆς λιθαργύρου τό τε ἔλαιον καὶ τὸ ὄξος. ἐὰν δὲ καὶ παλαιὸν ᾖ τὸ ἔλαιον, ἔτι καὶ μᾶλλον ξηραίνει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For that [sponge] makes the whole place wrinkled and contracted, not 401 only repelling what flows in, but also squeezing out what is contained within it toward the neighbouring parts. And the plaster now mentioned in our account would be said to dry not by its primary power, but accidentally. For properly and primarily it is the things that disperse and those that alter the temperaments toward the drier that are called drying; but accidentally also those that, through cooling, check what flows in and squeeze out what is contained. In general, none of the moistening things, whether primarily or accidentally, is beneficial for ulcers, as has been shown in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. [On the plaster prepared from litharge and vinegar-and-oil.] Concerning litharge prepared with water-and-oil (hydrelaion), then, this too is sufficient. It is now time to pass on to the one compounded with vinegar-and-oil (oxelaion). The drug becomes moderately drying if each of the two, the oil and the vinegar, is double the litharge. And if the oil is also old, it dries still more.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

For the sponge makes the whole place shrivelled and contracted, not 401 only repelling what flows in, but also squeezing out what is contained in it towards the neighbouring parts. And the plaster just described in the account might be said to dry not by primary power but incidentally. For properly and primarily, those things that disperse, and those that change the temperaments towards the drier, are called drying; but incidentally also those that, through cooling, check what flows in and squeeze out what is contained. In general, nothing that moistens, whether primarily or incidentally, is beneficial for ulcers, as has been shown in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. [On the plaster prepared with litharge and oxelaeum.] Concerning the litharge prepared with hydrelaeum, then, this too is sufficient. It is now time to pass on to the one compounded with oxelaeum (vinegar and oil). The drug, then, becomes moderately drying if the oil and the vinegar are each double the litharge. And if the oil is also old, it dries still more.

Greek (Kühn)

﻿ 402 πλέον δὲ ἢ διπλάσιον ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ἐμβληθὲν ἰσχυρότερον ποιεῖ τὸ φάρμακον, ὥστε καὶ σύριγγας, ὅσαι μήπω τύλον ἔχουσι σκληρὸν, ἰᾶσθαι. βάλλεται δὲ λιθαργύρου μὲν ἓν μέρος, ἑκατέρου δ’ ἐκείνων δύο καὶ ἥμισυ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τριπλάσιον ἐνίοτε μίξας ἑκατέρου, δι’ ὅλης μὲν ἡμέρας ἥψησα τὸ φάρμακον. ἐγένετο δὲ μέλαν μὲν, στίλβον δ’ ὁμοίως ἀσφάλτῳ τῇ καλῇ. ξηραντικωτάτην δ’ ἔχει δύναμιν τοῦτο, θεραπεύει τε ἕλκη δυσίατα καὶ σύριγγας οἵας εἶπον, ἐναίμων τε τραυμάτων κολλητικὸν ὑπάρχει. ἑνὶ δὲ λόγῳ ξηραντικὸν ἀδήκτως. διὰ τοῦτο οὖν καὶ τἄλλα πάντα ἕλκη θεραπεύει τά τε μείζω καὶ τὰ μικρότερα, μέχρι συνουλώσεως. ἄξιον δὲ καὶ ἄλλα τινὰ τῶν ἔργων αὐτοῦ διελθεῖν, ἃ τάχ’ ἂν οὐδὲ ἐλπίσειέ τις, ὅσον ἐπὶ τῷ τῆς ὕλης εὐκαταφρονήτῳ. κριθέντος γάρ τινος ἐν πυρετῷ διὰ παρωτίδος ἐκπυησάσης, ὑπελείφθη συρίγγιον, ὃ μετὰ μῆνας ἓξ τῆς ἀρχῆς ἐδείχθη μοι κατ’ ἀγρόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

402 But if more than double of each of them is put in, it makes the drug stronger, so that it heals even fistulas, as many as do not yet have a hard callus. One part of litharge is put in, and two and a half of each of those. And indeed, having sometimes mixed in triple of each, I boiled the drug through a whole day. It became black, but shining like fine asphalt. This has a most drying power, and it treats ulcers hard to heal and fistulas such as I mentioned, and it is agglutinative of bloody wounds. In a word, it is drying without biting. For this reason, therefore, it also treats all the other ulcers, both the greater and the smaller, up to cicatrization. It is worth going through some other of its works as well, which one would perhaps not even expect, so far as concerns the contemptible character of the material. For when someone in a fever had reached a crisis through a parotid swelling that suppurated, a small fistula was left behind, which was shown to me in the country six months after its beginning.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐναίμων, παρωτίδος, ὅσον ἐπὶ τῷ τῆς ὕλης εὐκαταφρονήτῳ

402 But if more than double of each of them is put in, it makes the drug stronger, so that it even heals fistulae, those that do not yet have a hard callus. One part of litharge is put in, and two and a half of each of the other two. And indeed I have sometimes mixed in triple of each and boiled the drug through a whole day. It became black, but glistening like good bitumen. This has a most drying power, and it treats ulcers that are hard to heal and fistulae of the kind I described, and it is agglutinative of bloody wounds. In a word, it is drying without biting. For this reason, then, it also treats all the other ulcers, both the larger and the smaller, up to cicatrisation. It is worth going through some other of its effects as well, which one would perhaps not even expect, considering how contemptible its materials are. For when someone had reached a crisis in a fever by way of a parotis that suppurated, a small fistula was left behind, which was shown to me in the country six months after its beginning.

Greek (Kühn)

ἦν γὰρ ὁ παῖς υἱὸς γεωργοῦ περὶ τὴν πέντε καὶ δέκα τὴν ἡλικίαν, τούτῳ δοὺς ἐπιτεθῆναι τῆς ἐμπλάστρου 403 ταύτης, ὅσον εἶχον ἑπόμενον, αὐτὸς μὲν ἐπανῆλθον εἰς τὴν πόλιν, ἐν ἀσχολίαις πλείοσιν ἰατρικαῖς γενόμενος ἐπελαθόμην τοῦ λαβόντος τὸ φάρμακον. ἀλλὰ μετὰ δύο γε μῆνας ἧκεν ὑγιὴς, οὐ πάνυ τι ἐλπίσαντος ἐμοῦ τοῦτο γενέσθαι. καθιέντων γὰρ ἡμῶν ὑείαν τρίχα διὰ τοῦ συριγγίου, μέχρι τῆς διαρθρώσεως ἐφαίνετο προερχομένη, κατὰ τύχην δὲ μετ’ ὀλίγας ἡμέρας ἄλλου μοι δειχθέντος παιδαρίου τὴν ἡλικίαν ἄγοντος τὴν αὐτὴν τῷ πρόσθεν, ὁμοίαν τε διάθεσιν ἔχοντος, ἐπέθηκα τῆς ἐμπλάστρου, καὶ συνέβη καὶ τούτῳ τελέως ὑγιασθῆναι. μετὰ ταῦτα δὲ οὐκ ὀλίγους ἐκ τῆς αὐτῆς διαθέσεως ἰασάμην τῷ φαρμάκῳ τούτῳ καὶ παῖδας καὶ γυναῖκας καὶ νεανίσκους. εἰ τοίνυν καὶ ὑμεῖς βούλεσθε χρῆσθαι πρὸς τὰ τοιαῦτα αὐτῷ, παλαιότατόν τε τὸ ἔλαιον ἐμβάλλετε καὶ ὄξος δριμύτατον ἐξ οἴνου δηλονότι μόνου γεγονὸς, οὐχ ὡς ἔνιοι σκευάζουσιν αὐτὸ μιγνύντες ὕδωρ. ἔστω δὲ καθαρὸν ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα καὶ διαφανὲς τὸ ὄξος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For the boy was a farmer's son, about fifteen years of age; having given him, to be applied, as much of this plaster 403 as I had with me, I myself returned to the city, and, being caught up in a good many medical engagements, I forgot about the one who had received the drug. But after two months he came back healthy, though I had not at all expected this to happen. For when we passed a pig's bristle through the little fistula, it appeared to advance as far as the articulation. By chance, however, a few days later another boy was shown to me, of the same age as the previous one and having a similar condition; I applied some of the plaster, and it came about that he too was completely restored to health. After this I cured not a few persons of the same condition with this drug—children and women and young men. If, then, you too wish to use it for such cases, put in the oldest oil and the sharpest vinegar, one made, clearly, from wine alone, not as some prepare it by mixing in water. And let the vinegar be as pure and transparent as possible.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅσον εἶχον ἑπόμενον

For the patient was a boy, the son of a farmer, about fifteen years of age. Having given him as much of this plaster 403 as I had with me, to be applied, I myself returned to the city and, being taken up with a great many medical engagements, forgot about the one who had received the drug. But after two months he came back healthy, although I had not at all expected this to happen. For when we let a pig's bristle down through the little fistula, it appeared to go in as far as the articulation. By chance, a few days later, another little boy was shown to me, of the same age as the previous one and having a similar condition; I applied the plaster, and it happened that this one too was completely healed. After this I cured with this drug not a few from the same condition, children, women and young men alike. If, then, you too wish to use it for such cases, put in the oldest oil and the sharpest vinegar, made, that is, from wine alone, not as some prepare it, mixing in water. Let the vinegar be as pure and transparent as possible.

οὕτω γὰρ ἂν εἴη λεπτομερέστερόν τε καὶ βέλτιον εἰς 404 ἃ δεόμεθα. τούτῳ τῷ φαρμάκῳ καὶ μῦς ἐσκληρυσμένους πολλάκις ἰασάμην. [Περὶ τῆς διὰ λιθαργύρου καὶ οἰνελαίου σκευαζομένης ἐμπλάστρου.] Ὑπόλοιπον οὖν μοι διελθεῖν περὶ τῆς δι’ οἰνελαίου σκευαζομένης λιθαργύρου, τὰ μὲν τῆς συμμετρίας ἐχούσης τὰ αὐτὰ τοῖς εἰρημένοις, ἀκολούθως τε τὰς τῆς δυνάμεως ὑπεροχὰς τῷ πλήθει τῶν ὑγρῶν. καὶ γὰρ ἴσα τὰ τρία βαλὼν ἐσκεύασα, καὶ διπλάσιον ἑκάτερον τῶν ὑγρῶν τῆς λιθαργύρου καὶ πρὸς τῷ διπλασίονι ἔτι τὸ ἥμισυ προσθεὶς καί ποτε καὶ τριπλάσιον. ἐσκεύασα δέ ποτ’ αὐτὸ καὶ οὕτως. ὥρᾳ θέρους ἐν ἡλίῳ θερμῷ καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν ἡ λιθάργυρος ἐτρίβετο μετ’ οἴνου Φαλερίνου καὶ τοῦτο ἡμέραις ἐγένετο ιε΄. προσεπιχέοντός μου πάλιν οἶνον, εἰ διαφορηθεὶς ἔτυχεν ὁ πρότερος. μετὰ δὲ τὰς εἰρημένας ἡμέρας ἑψηθὲν τὸ φάρμακον ἱκανῶς ἐγένετο ξηραντικόν. ἐχρησάμην δὲ αὐτῷ καὶ κατὰ τῶν ῥευματικῶν διαθέσεων ὁμοίως τῇ φοινικίνῃ διατήκων ἐλαίῳ, καθάπερ ἐπ’ ἐκείνης προείρηται.

For thus it would be more fine-particled and better for 404 the purposes we require. With this drug I have also often cured muscles that had become hardened. [On the plaster prepared from litharge and oinelaion (wine-and-oil).] It remains, then, for me to go through the litharge prepared with oinelaion, which in the matter of proportion has the same features as those already described, and correspondingly has the excesses of its power according to the quantity of the liquids. For I have prepared it putting in the three in equal amounts, and with each of the liquids double the litharge, and adding a further half beyond the double, and sometimes even triple. And I once prepared it also in the following way. In the summer season, in hot sun, each day the litharge was triturated with Falernian wine, and this went on for fifteen days, with me pouring in wine again if the previous wine happened to have been dispersed. After the said days the drug, once boiled, became sufficiently drying. I have also used it against rheumatic conditions in the same way as the phoinikine, melting it down with oil, just as has been said before in the case of that one.

καὶ μηρὸν δὲ χρονίως ἐσκληρυσμένον ἰασάμην αὐτῷ καὶ κατὰ 405 τραυμάτων ἐναίμων ἐπέθηκα καὶ ἦν εἰς πάντα κάλλιστον. ἔστω τοιγαροῦν ἐπ’ αὐτοῦ, κᾂν μὴ Φαλερῖνον ἔχῃ τις ἕτοιμον, ἕτερος κιῤῥὸς μὲν τὴν χρόαν, λεπτὸς δὲ τὴν σύστασιν ὡς διαυγεῖσθαι. τοιοῦτος δέ ἐστιν ὅ τ’ Ἀριούσιος ἐν τῇ Χίῳ γεωργούμενος, ὅ τ’ εὐώδης πάνυ Λέσβιος, ἐν Μιτυλήνῃ μὲν ὀλίγος, ἐν Ἐρεσσῷ δὲ καὶ Μηθύμνῃ πλείων τε καὶ βελτίων ἅμα γινόμενος, ὅ θ’ ὅμοιος τῷ Φαλερίνῳ Τμωλίτης. ἔστι δὲ καὶ οὗτος λεπτότατος καὶ διαυγὴς, κιῤῥὸς δὲ τὴν χρόαν καὶ μετρίως γλυκὺς εὐώδης τε πάνυ. τούτῳ παλαιωθέντι εἰς ἅπαντα χρῆσθαι δυνατόν ἐστιν, εἰς ἅπερ καὶ τῷ Φαλερίνῳ. καὶ ἕτερος δ’ ἐστὶ Τμωλίτης οἶνος, ὅμοιος αὐτῷ κατὰ τὴν χροιὰν καὶ τὴν σύστασιν, ἀλλ’ οὔτε γλυκὺς οὔτ’ εὐώδης. ἔχει δέ τινα στύψιν, ἀπολειπόμενος δὲ τοῦ γλυκέος πολύ. ταυτὶ μὲν οὖν ἑψομένης τῆς λιθαργύρου γίνεται φάρμακα. [Περὶ χρήσεως ὠμῆς λιθαργύρου.] Χωρὶς δὲ ἑψήσεως καθ’ ἑαυτὴν μὲν, ὡς ἔφην, ἐπὶ παρατριμμάτων ἐστὶ χρήσιμος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παρατρίμματα, κιῤῥὸς

And I also cured with it a thigh that had long been hardened, and I applied it to 405 bleeding wounds, and it was most excellent for everything. Accordingly, in its case, even if one does not have Falernian ready to hand, let there be another wine, tawny in colour but thin in consistency, so as to be translucent. Such is the Ariousian, cultivated on Chios, and the very fragrant Lesbian, which is produced in small quantity at Mitylene, but at Eresos and Methymna is at once both more abundant and better, and the Tmolite, which resembles the Falernian. This too is very thin and translucent, tawny in colour, moderately sweet and very fragrant. When it has been aged it is possible to use it for all the purposes for which the Falernian too is used. And there is also another Tmolite wine, similar to it in colour and consistency, but neither sweet nor fragrant; it has a certain astringency, but falls far short of the sweet one. These, then, are the drugs that are produced when the litharge is boiled. [On the use of raw litharge.] Without boiling, by itself, as I said, it is useful for chafings (παρατρίμματα).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παρατριμμάτων

With it I also cured a thigh that had become chronically hardened, and I applied it to 405 bloody wounds, and it proved excellent for everything. Accordingly, for this preparation, if one does not have Falernian at hand, let there be another wine, tawny in colour and thin in consistency, so as to be translucent. Of this kind is the Ariusian, grown on Chios, and the very fragrant Lesbian, of which little is produced at Mytilene, but more, and at the same time better, at Eresus and Methymna, and the Tmolite, which resembles the Falernian. This too is very thin and translucent, tawny in colour, moderately sweet and very fragrant. When it has been aged, it can be used for all the purposes for which Falernian is used. There is also another Tmolite wine, similar to it in colour and consistency, but neither sweet nor fragrant. It has a certain astringency, and falls far short of the sweet one. These, then, are the drugs that are made when the litharge is boiled. [On the use of raw litharge.] Without boiling, on its own, as I said, it is useful for chafings.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ δὲ προπαρασκευασθείη δι’ ὄξους ἢ οἴνου τριβεῖσα καὶ ξηρανθεῖσα, τῶν δι’ ὑγρότητα δυσεπουλώτων 406 ἑλκῶν ἴαμα γίνεται. καὶ εἴ γε βουληθείης ἄδηκτον αὐτὴν εἶναι, πλυτῇ χρήσῃ. [Ὅπως δεῖ πλύνειν λιθάργυρον καὶ μεταλλικὰ λοιπά.] Ῥᾳδίως δὲ πλύνεται διὰ τὸ σύμφυτον βάρος ὑφισταμένη ταχέως τοῖς ὑγροῖς, ἐν οἷς λειοῦται. τελεώτατα μὲν οὖν ἄδηκτος γίγνεται δι’ ὕδατος πλυθεῖσα. τοῖς δὲ εὐαισθήτοις σώμασι βραχεῖαν αἴσθησιν φέρει δήξεως, ὅταν δι’ ὄξους ἢ οἴνου πλυθῇ, χρὴ δὲ ἱκανῶς αὐτὴν προσλειώσαντα δι’ ὅλης ἡμέρας μετὰ τοῦ ὑγροῦ ἐᾶσαι καταστῆναι τῆς νυκτὸς, εἶτα κατὰ τὴν ὑστεραίαν ἕωθεν ἀποχέοντα τὸ ὑγρὸν αὖθις ἐπιχεῖν ἕτερον, εἶτ’ αὖθις ὁμοίως τρίβειν, ἀποχεῖν τε πάλιν ἐπὶ τῆς ὑστεραίας, καὶ τοῦτο ποιεῖν, ἄχρις ἂν ἅμα τῷ ὑγρῷ φαίνηταί τις οἷον ἄχνη τῆς λιθαργύρου. μηκέτι δ’ ἐφισταμένου μηδενὸς, οὐδ’ ἀποχεῖν ἔτι προσήκει, τρίβειν μέντοι πλείοσιν ἡμέραις, κᾂν μηδὲν ἐφίστηται, βέλτιόν ἐστι. καὶ τοῦτο πρᾶττε ἐν ἡλίῳ θερμῷ· ξηραντικώτερον γὰρ οὕτως τὸ φάρμακον γίγνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄχνη

But if it has been prepared beforehand by being ground with vinegar or wine and dried, it becomes a remedy for ulcers that are hard to cicatrize 406 on account of moisture. And if indeed you should wish it to be non-biting, you will use it washed. [How one should wash litharge and the remaining metallic substances.] It is washed easily, since on account of its innate heaviness it quickly settles in the liquids in which it is levigated. Now it becomes most completely non-biting when washed with water; but to sensitive bodies it brings a slight sensation of bitingness when it has been washed with vinegar or wine. One must, having first levigated it sufficiently with the liquid through a whole day, let it settle during the night; then on the following day, at dawn, pouring off the liquid, pour on another in its place; then again grind in the same way, and pour off again on the following day, and do this as long as some sort of fine scum of the litharge appears together with the liquid. But when nothing any longer floats on top, it is no longer fitting to pour off either; it is better, however, to grind for several more days, even if nothing floats on top. And do this in hot sun; for in this way the drug becomes more drying.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄχνη, ἐφισταμένου, ἐφίστηται

But if it is prepared beforehand by being ground with vinegar or wine and then dried, it becomes a remedy for ulcers that are hard to cicatrise 406 on account of moisture. And if you should wish it to be non-biting, you will use it washed. [How one must wash litharge and the other metallic substances.] It is washed easily because, owing to its natural weight, it quickly settles to the bottom in the liquids in which it is levigated. It becomes most completely non-biting when it has been washed with water. But to sensitive bodies it brings a slight sensation of biting when it has been washed with vinegar or wine. One must levigate it thoroughly with the liquid through the whole day and then let it settle during the night. Then, early on the following day, one pours off the liquid and pours on fresh liquid, grinds it again in the same way, and pours it off again on the following day. One does this until there appears, together with the liquid, something like a fine scum of the litharge. When nothing any longer collects on the surface, there is no further need to pour off. To go on grinding it for more days, however, even if nothing collects on the surface, is better. And do this in hot sun, for in this way the drug becomes more drying.

Greek (Kühn)

εὔδηλον δὲ ὅτι θέρους ὥρᾳ τοῦτο πράττειν ἄμεινον οὐκ ἐπὶ λιθαργύρου μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ﻿ 407 ἄλλων, ὅσα τὸν τρόπον τοῦτον παρασκευάζεται. καὶ γὰρ ἡ καδμεία καὶ ἡ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπὶς, ὅ τε κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς, ἔτι τε πρὸς τούτοις τὸ διφρυγὲς ὀνομαζόμενον καὶ τὸ ψιμύθιον καὶ τὸ μίσυ καὶ τὸ σῶρυ καὶ τὸ στίμμι καὶ πάντες οἱ λίθοι καὶ πρὸς αὐτοῖς ὁ τίτανος ὁμοίως τῷ λιθαργύρῳ καὶ πλύνεται καὶ λειοῦται μετά τινος τῶν εἰρημένων. ἐνίοτε δὲ καὶ διὰ θαλάττης αὐτῶν ποιοῦμαι τὴν παρασκευὴν περὶ ὧν αὖθις εἰρήσεται προϊόντος τοῦ λόγου. νυνὶ γὰρ πρόκειταί μοι τὰς ἐμπλάστρους διελθεῖν, ἃς οἱ νεώτεροι τῶν ἰατρῶν μετὰ τοῦ ρω. γράφουσί τε καὶ λέγουσιν ἐπὶ τῆς ὑστάτης συλλαβῆς. ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τοῦ κεντρίου καὶ τῆς μηλωτρίδος εἰώθασι ποιεῖν ἅπαντες. καὶ γὰρ ἐπὶ τούτων ἡ μὲν πρώτη θέσις τῶν ὀνομάτων ἔοικεν ἄνευ τοῦ ρω γεγονέναι, παρὰ μὲν τὸ κεντεῖν ὠνομασμένου τοῦ κεντρίου τῆς ἐσχάτης συλλαβῆς οὐκ ἐχούσης τὸ ρω, τῆς μηλωτρίδος δὲ συγκειμένης ἐκ μήλης τε καὶ ὠτός. νυνὶ μέντοι σχεδὸν ἁπάντων αὐτὰ μετὰ τοῦ ρω λεγόντων οὐκ ἂν ἁμάρτοι τις ὁμοίως αὐτοῖς φθεγγόμενος καὶ μάλιστα τὸ κέντριον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διφρυγὲς, σῶρυ

It is quite evident that it is better to do this in the summer season, not only in the case of litharge but also of the 407 others, all those which are prepared in this manner. For cadmia and the scale of copper and burnt copper, and further, in addition to these, the so-called diphryges and white lead and misy and sory and stimmi and all the stones, and besides these lime, are, in the same way as litharge, both washed and levigated with one of the liquids mentioned. Sometimes I also carry out their preparation with sea-water; about these things more will be said again as the discourse proceeds. For at present my task is to go through the emplastra, which the more recent physicians both write and pronounce with the rho in the last syllable, just as all are accustomed to do in the case of the kentrion and the melotris. For in the case of these too the original imposition of the names seems to have taken place without the rho, the kentrion having been named from kentein (to prick), its final syllable not having the rho, and the melotris being compounded from mele (probe) and ous (ear). Now, however, since nearly everyone pronounces them with the rho, one would not err in uttering them in the same way as they do, and especially kentrion.

It is quite clear that it is better to do this in the summer season, not only in the case of litharge but also of the 407 other substances that are prepared in this manner. For cadmia, the flake of copper, burnt copper, and besides these what is called diphryges, white lead, misy, sory, stimmi, all the stones, and in addition to these titanos (lime), are both washed and ground smooth in the same way as litharge, with one of the liquids mentioned. Sometimes I also carry out their preparation with sea water; about these matters more will be said later as the discourse proceeds. For at present my task is to go through the plasters (emplastroi), which the more recent physicians both write and pronounce with the rho in the last syllable, just as everyone is accustomed to do in the case of the kentrion and the mēlōtris (ear-probe). For in these cases too the original imposition of the names seems to have been without the rho: the kentrion was named from kentein ('to prick'), whose last syllable does not have the rho, and the mēlōtris is compounded of mēlē (probe) and ous, ōtos (ear). Now, however, since almost everyone pronounces them with the rho, one would not err in uttering them as they do, and especially the kentrion.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπιδέδεικται 408 γάρ μοι κατὰ τὰ πρὸς τοὺς ἐπιτιμῶντας τοῖς σολοικίζουσι καὶ αὐτοὺς Ἀττικοὺς ἄνδρας ἠκολουθηκέναι τῇ κρατούσῃ συνηθείᾳ. δέδεικται γὰρ καὶ ἄλλοις πρὸ ἐμοῦ τῆς Ἀτθίδος αὐτῆς διαλέκτου μετάπτωσις γεγονέναι πολυειδὴς, ἕπεσθαί τε τῷ καθ’ ἑαυτοὺς ἔθει πάντας, ὧν δόξα μεγίστη παρὰ τοῖς Ἕλλησίν ἐστιν ἐπὶ λόγων δεινότητι. καὶ ἡμεῖς οὖν, ἐπειδὴ πάσας εὑρίσκομεν ἤδη τὰς φαρμακίτιδας βίβλους μετὰ τοῦ ρω γεγραμμένον ἐχούσας τοὔνομα τὸ τῆς ἐμπλάστρου, καὶ αὐτοὶ τῇ κρατούσῃ συνηθείᾳ χρησόμεθα. [Περὶ τῶν διὰ μολυβδαίνης σκευαζομένων φαρμάκων.] Εἴρηταί μοι καὶ πρόσθεν ὡς ἡ τῆς μολυβδαίνης χρεία παραπλησία ἐστὶ τῇ τῆς λιθαργύρου. πάντα γὰρ ὅσα διὰ λιθαργύρου συντίθενται καὶ διὰ μολυβδαίνης δύναται σκευάζεσθαι. τῇ δυνάμει δὲ ἀλλήλων διαφέρουσι τοσοῦτον, ὡς παχυμερεστέραν μὲν εἶναι καὶ ψυχροτέραν τὴν οὐσίαν τῆς μολυβδαίνης, λεπτομερεστέραν δὲ καὶ μέσην θερμοῦ τε καὶ ψυχροῦ τὴν τῆς λιθαργύρου.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For it has been shown 408 by me, in my writings against those who censure people who commit solecisms, that the Attic men themselves too followed the prevailing usage. For it has been shown also by others before me that a manifold change of the Attic dialect itself has taken place, and that all those whose reputation among the Greeks for skill in speech is greatest follow the custom of their own time. And so we too, since we now find all the books on drugs having the name of the emplastron written with the rho, shall ourselves also follow the prevailing usage. [On the drugs prepared with molybdaena.] It has been said by me also earlier that the use of molybdaena is closely similar to that of litharge. For everything that is compounded with litharge can also be prepared with molybdaena. In their power they differ from one another to this extent, that the substance of molybdaena is more coarse-particled and colder, while that of litharge is more fine-particled and intermediate between hot and cold.

For I have shown 408 in my work Against Those Who Censure People Who Commit Solecisms that even the Attic men themselves followed the prevailing usage. Indeed, it has also been shown by others before me that the Attic dialect itself has undergone a manifold change, and that all those who have the greatest reputation among the Greeks for skill in speech follow the custom of their own time. We too, then, since we find that all the pharmaceutical books now have the name of the plaster (emplastros) written with the rho, shall ourselves also follow the prevailing usage. [On the drugs prepared with molybdaena.] I have also said before that the use of molybdaena is closely similar to that of litharge. For all the drugs that are compounded with litharge can also be prepared with molybdaena. In potency they differ from one another only to this extent: the substance of molybdaena is thicker-particled and colder, while that of litharge is finer-particled and intermediate between hot and cold.

Greek (Kühn)

πρὸς δὲ τὰς χρόας τῶν φαρμάκων, ὅσα δι’ αὐτῶν σκευάζεται, διαφορά τις 409 αὐτῆς ἐστὶ, καθόσον ἐπὶ τὸ φαιότερον ἄγει τὰς χρόας ἡ μολύβδαινα. διὸ καὶ τῶν λευκῶν ἐμπλάστρων οὐ μόνον ψιμύθιον, ἀλλὰ καὶ λιθάργυρον ἐχουσῶν, οὐδεμία διὰ μολυβδαίνης σκευάζεται. μιχθείσης γὰρ αὐτῆς τὸ μὲν λευκὸν χρῶμα τεφρῶδες γίγνεται. τὰ δὲ τῆς δυνάμεως οὐ μόνον οὐκ ἀπόλλυται κατά γε τὴν δι’ ὑδρελαίου σκευαζομένην ἔμπλαστρον λευκὴν, ἀλλὰ καὶ βελτίω γίνεται καὶ μάλιστα ἐπὶ τῶν μαλακοσάρκων. ἡνίκα δὲ διαφορῆσαί τι βουλόμεθα, βελτίων ἐστὶ μολυβδαίνης λιθάργυρος. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων φαρμάκων ἁπάντων, ὅσα διὰ λιθαργύρου γέγραπται συντιθέμενα, βαλλόντων τὴν μολύβδαιναν, ἥ τε χρόα τοῦ φαρμάκου καὶ ἡ δύναμις εἰς τοσοῦτον ἔχει τὴν ἀλλοίωσιν, εἰς ὅσον ἄρτι μοι δεδήλωται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But with respect to the colours of the drugs that are prepared by means of them, there is a certain difference 409 in it, inasmuch as molybdaina leads the colours toward the greyer. For this reason too, of the white plasters that contain not only white lead but also litharge, none is prepared with molybdaina; for when it has been mixed in, the white colour becomes ash-coloured. The properties of the power, however, not only are not lost—at least in the case of the white plaster prepared with water-and-oil (hydrelaion)—but even become better, and especially in the case of those with soft flesh. But when we wish to disperse something, litharge is better than molybdaina. So too with all the other drugs which have been recorded as being compounded with litharge: if one puts in molybdaina, both the colour of the drug and its power undergo alteration to the degree that has just been shown by me.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐτῆς, φαιότερον, μαλακοσάρκων

With respect to the colours of the drugs prepared with them, there is a certain difference 409 in it, inasmuch as molybdaena brings the colours toward the duskier. For this reason, of the white plasters which contain not only white lead but also litharge, none is prepared with molybdaena. For when it is mixed in, the white colour becomes ash-coloured. But what pertains to the potency is not only not lost, at least in the white plaster prepared with hydrelaeum (water and oil), but actually becomes better, and especially in the case of soft-fleshed persons. When, however, we wish to disperse something, litharge is better than molybdaena. So too, in all the other drugs that are recorded as compounded with litharge, if one puts in molybdaena, both the colour of the drug and its potency undergo alteration to the extent that I have just set out.

Greek (Kühn)

[Περὶ τῶν διὰ λιθαργύρου καὶ ψιμυθίου σκευαζομένων λευκῶν ἐμπλάστρων.] Ἤτοι τῆς χροιᾶς ἕνεκεν οἱ ἰατροὶ μίξαντες ψιμύθιον τῇ λιθαργύρῳ σκευάζουσι τὰς λευκὰς ἐμπλάστρους, ἢ χάριν τοῦ στύψιν καὶ ψύξιν τινὰ προσθεῖναι τῷ φαρμάκῳ, θεώμενοι δὲ καὶ μελαινόμενον, 410 ὅταν ἐπὶ πλέον ἕψηται τὸ τοιοῦτο, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἐπὶ ξύλων οὐδὲ τούτων ἴσως ἀκάπνων οὐδὲ ἐν ἀέρι καθαρῷ, ταχέως μὲν αἴρειν ἀπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς ἀναγκάζονται, δι’ αὐτὸ δὲ τοῦτο μήθ’ ὕδωρ δαψιλὲς μήτ’ ἔλαιον μιγνύειν. ἐπενόησαν οὖν εἰκότως ἐπεμβάλλειν αὐτῷ ῥητίνης, ὅπως ὅλκιμόν τε καὶ συνεχὲς εἴη, προσλαμβάνον τινὰ γλισχρότητα, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἡ ὑγρὰ ῥητίνη μιχθῇ. χεῖρον δὲ γίνεται τὸ φάρμακον ἐπὶ τῇ τοιαύτῃ μίξει, δακνῶδες μέν τι καὶ ῥητίνης ἐχούσης, τὸ ξηραντικὸν δὲ οὐ πάνυ τι γενναῖον, οὗ μάλιστα χρείαν εἶναι τοῖς ἕλκεσιν ἐμάθετε. τίνες μὲν οὖν ῥητίναι καὶ πάνυ δακνώδεις εἰσὶ καὶ ῥηθήσεται περὶ πασῶν αὐτῶν αὖθις. αἱ πρᾳόταται δὲ ἐν αὐταῖς δύο εἰσί. πρώτη μὲν ἡ τερμινθίνη, δευτέρα δὲ ἡ λάριξ ὀνομαζομένη. τούτων οὖν ἄμεινον μιγνύειν, ὅταν τις βουληθῇ τοιαύτην ἔμπλαστρον σκευάσαι. πολὺ δ’ εὐαφέστερα γίγνεται πάντα, τά τε διὰ ῥητίνης σκευαζόμενα προσλαμβάνοντα κηροῦ τι, τά τε διὰ κηροῦ μιχθείσης αὐτοῖς ῥητίνης. ἐπεμβάλλειν οὖν χρὴ τῇ ῥητίνῃ καὶ κηρόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τίνες

[On the white plasters prepared with litharge and white lead.] Either for the sake of the colour physicians prepare the white plasters by mixing white lead with the litharge, or for the sake of adding some astringency and cooling to the drug; and seeing that such a drug also turns black 410 when it is boiled for too long, and especially when over wood—and that perhaps not even smokeless—and not in clean air, they are compelled to remove it quickly from the fire, and for this very reason to mix in neither abundant water nor oil. They therefore reasonably devised adding resin to it, so that it might be ductile and cohesive, acquiring a certain viscosity, and especially when the liquid resin is mixed in. But the drug becomes worse through such a mixture, since resin too has something biting, while its drying property is not at all vigorous—and you have learnt that ulcers have especial need of this. Some resins, then, are even very biting, and all of them will be discussed again later. The gentlest among them are two: first the terebinth, and second the one called larch. It is better, then, to mix in some of these when one wishes to prepare such a plaster. And all become much more pleasant to the touch, both those prepared with resin when they take on some wax, and those prepared with wax when resin is mixed in with them. One must therefore add wax too along with the resin.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅλκιμόν, εὐαφέστερα, γενναῖον, μελαινόμενον

[On the white plasters prepared with litharge and white lead.] Physicians mix white lead with litharge in preparing the white plasters either for the sake of the colour, or in order to add some astringency and cooling to the drug. But they see that such a preparation also turns black 410 when it is boiled for longer, and especially when it is boiled over wood, perhaps not smokeless wood either, and not in clean air. They are therefore compelled to take it off the fire quickly, and for this very reason to mix in neither abundant water nor oil. So they reasonably thought of adding resin to it, so that it might be ductile and coherent by taking on a certain viscosity, especially when the liquid resin is mixed in. But the drug becomes worse with such a mixture, since resin has something biting about it, while its drying property is not at all strong, and you have learned that this property is what ulcers most need. Which resins are very biting will be stated again when all of them are discussed. The mildest among them are two: first the terebinth, second the one called larch. It is therefore better to mix these in whenever anyone wishes to prepare such a plaster. All things become much softer to the touch, both those prepared with resin when they take on some wax, and those prepared with wax when resin is mixed with them. One must therefore add wax as well to the resin.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅπως δὲ τὸ χρῶμα τῆς ἐμπλάστρου 411 λευκὸν φυλάττοιτο, λευκὸν εἶναι προσήκει τὸν κηρόν. ἔστι δὲ λευκὸς αὐτοφυὴς μὲν ὁ Ποντικὸς, ὀνομάζεται γὰρ οὕτως συνήθως ἤδη κᾂν ἐξ ἑτέρου τινὸς ᾖ χωρίου, διὰ τὸ πλεῖστον μὲν ἐν τῷ Πόντῳ γίγνεσθαι τὸν λευκὸν, ἐλάχιστον δὲ ἐν ἑτέροις χωρίοις. οὐκ αὐτοφυὴς δὲ λευκὸς ὁ Τυῤῥηνικὸς ὠνομασμένος ἐστίν. οὗτος μὲν οὖν οὐδεμίαν ἔχει σαφῆ δριμύτητα. τῶν αὐτοφυῶν δὲ ἔνιοι δριμεῖς εἰσιν, οὓς χρὴ πλύνειν πρότερον. ἡ πλύσις δ’ αὐτῶν γίγνεται τακέντων ἐπὶ πυρὸς, εἶτ’ εἰς ὕδωρ πηγαῖον καθαρὸν ἐμβληθέντων, ἀνακινουμένων τε ἐνταῦθα καὶ διαῤῥυπτομένων. οὕτω καὶ πίτταν καὶ ῥητίνην καὶ τὸ καλούμενον φύσημα πλυτέον ἐστὶ, περὶ ὧν αὖθις ἀκριβέστερον εἰρήσεται. νυνὶ δὲ ἐπειδὴ τῆς λευκῆς ἐμπλάστρου τὴν σύνθεσιν γράφω, τὸ κατάλοιπον ὧν εἶπον ἐφεξῆς διηγήσομαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φύσημα

And in order that the colour of the plaster 411 may be preserved white, the wax ought to be white. Naturally white is the so-called Pontic wax—for it is now customarily so named even if it comes from some other region, because white wax is produced in the greatest quantity in Pontus and in the least in other regions. Not naturally white is the wax named Tyrrhenian. This one, then, has no evident acridity; but some of the naturally white kinds are acrid, and these must first be washed. Their washing is done by melting them over a fire, then throwing them into clean spring water, and stirring them about there and rinsing them thoroughly. In the same way pitch and resin and what is called physema must also be washed; about these a more precise account will be given later. But now, since I am writing the composition of the white plaster, I shall relate in order the remainder of what I mentioned.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φύσημα, διαῤῥυπτομένων

And in order that the colour of the plaster 411 may be kept white, the wax ought to be white. White by nature is the Pontic wax; for it is now customarily called so even if it comes from some other place, because white wax is produced in the greatest quantity in Pontus and in the smallest quantity in other places. White, but not by nature, is the wax called Tyrrhenian. This one, then, has no evident acridity; but of the naturally white kinds some are acrid, and these must be washed first. Their washing is done as follows: they are melted over a fire, then thrown into clean spring water, and there stirred about and thoroughly cleansed. In this way pitch, resin and the so-called physema are also to be washed; about these a more exact account will be given later. But now, since I am writing out the composition of the white plaster, I shall next relate the remainder of what I was saying.

Greek (Kühn)

κηροῦ γὰρ ἐπεμβληθέντος ἅμα τῆ ῥητίνῃ προεψημένοις τῷ τε ψιμυθίῳ καὶ τῇ λιθαργύρῳ, μέχρις ἂν ἀκριβῶς ἀμόλυντον γένηται τὸ φάρμακον, ἔχοιτ’ ἂν ἤδη λευκὴν ἔμπλαστρον, εἰ βούλεσθε, ποτὲ μὲν ὕδατος ἐμβάλλοντες αὐτῇ, ποτὲ δὲ ἐλαίου μόνου, καὶ τούτου ποτὲ ﻿ 412 μὲν νέου, ποτὲ δὲ παλαιοῦ. προακηκόατε γὰρ ἤδη τίνα δύναμιν ὕδωρ καὶ παλαιὸν ἔλαιον ἔχει. γιγνώσκετε δὲ καὶ ὡς στύψεώς τι προσδίδωσι τῷ φαρμάκῳ, τό τ’ ὀμφάκινον ἔλαιον καὶ τὸ κατὰ τὴν Ἰβηρίαν γεωργούμενον, ἣν νῦν ὀνομάζουσιν Ἱσπανίαν. τὸ δὲ Ἰστρικὸν οὐ μόνον τὴν στύψιν ἔχει βραχεῖαν, ἀλλὰ καὶ πικρότητα. διαφορὰν οὖν ἕξουσιν αἱ διὰ τῶν στυφόντων σκευασίαι τῶν διὰ τοῦ γλυκέος ἐλαίου, καθόσον ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν καὶ τελευτῇ τῶν ἑλκῶν ἀμείνω πάντα ἐστὶ τὰ στύψεως ἔχοντά τι, κατὰ δὲ τὰ μέσα τὰ γλυκέα. δεῖται γὰρ ἐν μὲν ταῖς ἀρχαῖς τὰ ἕλκη τῶν ἀποκρουομένων φαρμάκων, ὅπως μηδὲ ἄρξηται φλεγμαίνειν. ἐφεξῆς δὲ τῶν χαλαστικωτέρων, ἐὰν μὲν μὴ γένηται φλεγμονὴ, χάριν τοῦ διαπνεῦσαι, φθασάσης δὲ γενέσθαι καὶ δι’ ἐκείνης ὅπως θᾶττον λυθείη. εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι περὶ σμικρᾶς φλεγμονῆς ὁ λόγος ἐστίν. οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται βραχέσιν, ἐφ’ ὧν χρώμεθα ταῖς λευκαῖς ταύταις ἐμπλάστροις, ἐπιγίνεσθαι μεγάλη φλεγμονὴ μηδενὸς ἁμαρτήματος προσελθόντος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀμόλυντον

For when wax has been added, together with the resin, to the white lead and the litharge that have been boiled beforehand, until the drug becomes thoroughly non-staining, you would now have a white plaster, if you wish, sometimes adding water to it, sometimes oil alone, and this sometimes 412 new, sometimes old. For you have already heard beforehand what power water and old oil have. And you know also that both omphacine oil and that cultivated in Iberia, which they now call Hispania, impart something of astringency to the drug. The Istrian oil has not only slight astringency but also bitterness. The preparations made with the astringent oils will therefore have a difference from those made with the sweet oil, insofar as at the beginning and at the end of ulcers all those that have something of astringency are better, but in the middle stages the sweet ones. For at the beginnings ulcers need the repellent drugs, so that they may not even begin to become inflamed; and thereafter the more relaxing ones: if inflammation does not occur, for the sake of transpiration; but if it has already occurred, also on account of that, so that it may be resolved more quickly. It is quite evident that the account concerns a small inflammation. For a great inflammation cannot supervene upon small ulcers, for which we use these white plasters, when no error has been committed in addition.

For you have already heard what power water and old oil have. You also know that both omphacine oil and the oil cultivated in Iberia, which they now call Hispania, impart something of astringency to the drug. For at the beginnings ulcers need repellent drugs, so that they do not even begin to become inflamed; and after that they need the more relaxing ones: if inflammation does not occur, for the sake of transpiration, but if it has already occurred, on account of it too, so that it may be resolved more quickly. It is quite clear that the discussion concerns a small inflammation. For in small ulcers, on which we use these white plasters, a large inflammation cannot supervene unless some error has been committed.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅτι δὲ καὶ συνουλουμένων ἤδη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἡ στύφουσα ποιότης ὠφέλιμός ἐστιν 413 οὐδεὶς ἀγνοεῖ. καιρὸς οὖν ἤδη τὴν συμμετρίαν εἰπεῖν τῶν συντεθέντων φαρμάκων τὴν χρησιμωτάτην ἔμπλαστρον λευκήν. [Ἡ σταθμεία τῆς ἐμπλάστρου.] Ἴσον ἔστω μέρος ἑκατέρου τῶν μεταλλικῶν, τοῦ τε ψιμυθίου καὶ τοῦ λιθαργύρου, καὶ τοῦτ’ εἰ βούλεσθε, σταθμὸς ἔστω μιᾶς λίτρας ἑκατέρου. συναμφοτέρων δὲ ἴσον ἐλαίου μέτρον ἢ ὡς ὀνομάζουσι Ῥωμαῖοι, λίτραι δύο, τοῦ κηροῦ δὲ ἡμίλιτρον ἔστω καὶ τῆς τερμινθίνης ἥμισυ, τουτέστιν οὐγγίας τρεῖς ὁ σταθμός. εἰ δὲ καὶ λιβανωτοῦ μίξαις, ἀνωδυνώτερόν τε καὶ πεπτικώτερον τῶν φλεγμαινόντων ἑλκυδρίων ἐργάσῃ τὸ φάρμακον, ἀλλὰ πρός γε τὴν ἐπούλωσιν ἀτονώτερον. ὑπερσαρκώσεις γὰρ, ἐπειδὴ σαρκωτικῶν ἐστιν ὁ λιβανωτὸς, ὡς κᾀν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου μεμαθήκατε. βέλτιον οὖν ἐστιν, ἐμβαλεῖν τι καὶ στυπτηρίας ὀλίγον ἕνεκά τε τῆς ἐπουλώσεως καὶ ἄλλως, ἐπειδὴ τὰ χαλαστικὰ φάρμακα πληθωρικοῖς τε καὶ κακοχύμοις σώμασι προσφερόμενα προτρέπει ῥευματίζεσθαι τὰ πεπονθότα μόρια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σταθμεία

And that the astringent quality is also beneficial when the ulcers are already cicatrizing, 413 no one is unaware. It is therefore now time to state the proportion of the drugs compounded into the most useful white plaster. [The weighing of the plaster.] Let there be an equal part of each of the two metallic substances, the white lead and the litharge, and this, if you wish, let the weight be one litra of each; and of oil, a measure equal to both together, or, as the Romans name it, two litrae; and of wax let there be half a litra, and of terebinth half of that, that is, a weight of three ounces. And if you should also mix in frankincense, you will make the drug more pain-relieving and more concoctive of small ulcers that are inflamed, but weaker, at any rate, with respect to cicatrization. For you will produce excess flesh, since frankincense belongs among the flesh-producing drugs, as you have also learned in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. It is therefore better to add also a little alum, both for the sake of cicatrization and for another reason, since relaxing drugs, when applied to plethoric bodies and to bodies full of bad humours, incite the affected parts to be subject to fluxes.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σταθμεία, τῶν συντεθέντων φαρμάκων τὴν χρησιμωτάτην ἔμπλαστρον λευκήν, ῥευματίζεσθαι

That the astringent quality is also beneficial when the ulcers are already cicatrizing, 413 no one is unaware. It is time, then, to state the proportion of the drugs that are compounded into the most useful white plaster. [The weighing-out of the plaster.] Let there be an equal part of each of the metallic substances, the white lead and the litharge, and, if you wish, let this be a weight of one litra of each. Of oil let there be a measure equal to both together, or, as the Romans call it, two litrae; of the wax let there be a half-litra, and of the terebinth half of that, that is, a weight of three ounces. If you also mix in frankincense, you will make the drug more anodyne and more concoctive of inflamed small ulcers, but weaker as regards cicatrization. For you will produce excess flesh, since frankincense is among the flesh-producing drugs, as you have also learned in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. It is better, then, to put in a little alum as well, both for the sake of cicatrization and for another reason: relaxing drugs, when applied to plethoric and cacochymic bodies, prompt the affected parts to suffer flux.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ δ’ ἐκ τούτων ἁπάντων συντεθὲν φάρμακον δυνήσεται διαφορεῖν ὄγκους 414 παρὰ φύσιν ἐπὶ τῶν ἁπαλοσάρκων, ὅσοι μήτε μεγάλοι λίαν εἰσὶ μήτ’ ὀδυνώδεις καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν λάβῃ δαψιλῆ τὸν λιβανωτόν. εἴη δ’ ἂν ἐπὶ τοῖς προειρημένοις δαψιλὴς ὁ λιβανωτὸς, ἐὰν ἴσος ᾖ κατὰ τὸν σταθμὸν τῇ τερμινθίνῃ. εἰ δὲ ἥμισυς ὁ λιβανωτὸς εἴη τῆς ῥητίνης, ἧττον μὲν ἔσται διαφορητικὸν τὸ φάρμακον, ἐπουλωτικώτερον δὲ τοῦ διπλάσιον ἔχοντος τὸν λιβανωτόν. εἰ δέ τι μᾶλλον ἐργάσασθαι διαφορητικὸν αὐτὸ βουληθείητε καὶ πεπέρεως ἐμβάλλετε τοῦ λευκοῦ βραχύ τι, μηδὲν εἰς τὴν ἐπούλωσιν τῶν ἑλκῶν βλάψαι δυναμένου. καὶ τοίνυν ἔστω τῆς μὲν στυπτηρίας γο. σταθμὸς, ἥμισυ δὲ τοῦ πεπέρεως. εὔλογον οὖν τούτου μὲν ἐμβληθῆναι τρεῖς δραχμὰς, ἓξ δὲ τῆς στυπτηρίας. ἐπεὶ δὲ καὶ ἄλλων ἐστὶ βελτίων τῶν στυπτηριῶν ἡ σχιστὴ καλουμένη καὶ τῇ χρόᾳ λευκὴ, ταύτης ἐμβάλλειν προσήκει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: γο.

The drug composed of all these will be able to disperse swellings 414 contrary to nature in persons of tender flesh, such swellings as are neither very large nor painful, and especially when it receives frankincense in abundance. The frankincense would be abundant, in relation to the ingredients mentioned above, if it is equal in weight to the terebinth. But if the frankincense were half of the resin, the drug will be less discutient, but more cicatrizing than the one containing double the frankincense. And if you should wish to make it somewhat more discutient, add also a little white pepper, since it is able to do no harm to the cicatrization of the ulcers. Accordingly, let the weight of the alum be an ounce (γο.), and that of the pepper half. It is reasonable, then, that three drachms of the latter be put in, and six of alum. And since the alum called split (σχιστή), white in colour, is better than the other alums, it is fitting to put in this kind.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο., ἐπὶ τοῖς προειρημένοις

The drug compounded from all of these will be able to disperse unnatural swellings 414 in those with soft flesh, such as are neither very large nor painful, and especially when it receives an abundant quantity of frankincense. And the frankincense would be abundant, given the quantities stated above, if it is equal in weight to the terebinth. But if the frankincense is half of the resin, the drug will be less dispersive, but more cicatrizing than the one that has double the frankincense. And if you should wish to make it somewhat more dispersive, then add also a little of the white pepper, which can do no harm at all to the cicatrization of the ulcers. So then let the weight of the alum be γο. [an abbreviated weight, apparently corrupt], and half that of the pepper. It is reasonable, then, that three drachms of the latter be added, and six of the alum. And since, among the alums, the one called split, which is also white in colour, is better than the others, it is fitting to add this one.

Greek (Kühn)

[Λευκὴ ἡ διὰ τοῦ λευκοῦ πεπέρεως, ὡς Ἄτταλος καὶ Ἥρας.] Ὠφελεῖ γερόντων ἕλκη καὶ ἁπαλοχρώτων καὶ πᾶν ἕλκος ἰᾶται, ὅσα ὑγρότερα καὶ δυσεπούλωτα καὶ ἀποσύρματα, ὡς ἀποκρέμασθαι τὸ δέρμα, ἐνίοτε 415 μελαινόμενον ἐπ’ ὠλεκράνου καὶ ἀντικνημίου καὶ γόνατος, ἐφ’ ὧν οὐδὲ σὰρξ, ἀλλ’ ὁ περιόστιος ὑμὴν ὑπόκειται μόνος. γέγονε τοίνυν πάντα ἐφεξῆς καταλέγοντι τὸ φάρμακον ἐκ τῶνδε συγκείμενον. λιθαργύρου μὲν καὶ ψιμυθίου, λίτρας ἑκατέρου σταθμῷ, δυοῖν δὲ λιτρῶν ἐλαίου μέτρῳ. καλεῖται γὰρ ὑπὸ Ῥωμαίων ὁμωνύμως ὁ λιτραῖος σταθμὸς τῶν στεῤῥῶν σωμάτων τῷ λιτραίῳ μέτρῳ τῶν ὑγρῶν, ὃ πάμπολυ καθ’ ὅλην τὴν πόλιν ἐστὶν, ἐξ ὕλης κερατίνης γιγνόμενον. ἐπὶ τούτοις δὲ τοῦ κηροῦ μὲν ἡμίλιτρον τῷ σταθμῷ, τριῶν δὲ οὐγγιῶν τῆς τερμινθίνης ῥητίνης, λιβανωτοῦ δὲ ἤτοι τοῦ ἡμίσεος ἢ τοῦ ἴσου ταύτῃ, καὶ στυπτηρίας μὲν ἓξ δραχμῶν, τριῶν δὲ τοῦ πεπέρεως. ἡ δὲ ἕψησις εὔδηλος ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς μὲν ἑψομένων τῶν μεταλλικῶν ἐν ἡλίῳ, μέχρι τοῦ γενέσθαι τελέως ἀμόλυντον ἢ καὶ βραχὺ σκληρότερον. ἐμβαλλομένων δὲ τηνικαῦτα κηροῦ καὶ ῥητίνης, εἶθ’ ὅταν ἑνωθῇ πάντα, τοῦ λιβανωτοῦ μὲν πρῶτον, ἐπ’ αὐτῷ δὲ τῆς στυπτηρίας τε καὶ τοῦ πεπέρεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποσύρματα, ἀμόλυντον

[White plaster, the one made with white pepper, as Attalus and Heras give it.] It benefits the ulcers of old men and of those with tender skin, and it heals every ulcer, those that are rather moist and hard to cicatrize, and abrasions (ἀποσύρματα), such that the skin hangs loose, sometimes 415 turning black, on the elbow and the shin and the knee, in which places there is not even flesh, but only the periosteal membrane lies beneath. So then, for one who enumerates everything in order, the drug has turned out to be composed of the following: of litharge and white lead, a litra of each by weight, and two litrai of oil by measure. For among the Romans the litra weight of solid bodies is called by the same name as the litra measure of liquids, which is very common throughout the whole city, being made of horn material. In addition to these, half a litra of wax by weight, three ounces of terebinth resin, of frankincense either half of this or an amount equal to it, and six drachmas of alum and three of pepper. The boiling is obvious: from the beginning the metallic ingredients are boiled in the sun, until it becomes completely non-staining (ἀμόλυντον) or even a little harder; then at that point wax and resin are thrown in, and then, when everything has been unified, first the frankincense, and after it the alum and the pepper.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποσύρματα, ἀμόλυντον, γέγονε τοίνυν πάντα ἐφεξῆς καταλέγοντι, ἐν ἡλίῳ

[The white (plaster) made with white pepper, as Attalus and Heras (prepare it).] It benefits the ulcers of old people and of those with soft skin, and it heals every ulcer, those that are rather moist and hard to cicatrise, and excoriations, such that the skin hangs loose, sometimes 415 blackening, on the point of the elbow, the shin and the knee, where there is not even flesh beneath, but only the periosteal membrane. Accordingly, for one listing everything in order, the drug turns out to be composed of the following: of litharge and of white lead, a litra of each by weight, and of oil two litrai by measure. For among the Romans the litra-weight of solid bodies is called by the same name as the litra-measure of liquids, which is found in great numbers throughout the whole city, being made of horn. In addition to these, of wax a half-litra by weight, of terebinth resin three ounces, of frankincense either half as much as this or an equal amount, and of alum six drachmas, and of the pepper three. The boiling is evident: first the metallic ingredients are boiled in the sun until the mixture becomes completely non-staining, or even a little harder; then at that point the wax and the resin are put in, and then, when everything has been united, first the frankincense, and after it the alum and the pepper.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐμβάλλειν δὲ χρὴ τὸ ῥοδιακὸν καλούμενον ψιμύθιον, εἰ βούλοιτό τις πάνυ λευκὴν τὴν 416 ἔμπλαστρον γενέσθαι. ἀλλὰ καὶ κατὰ τὴν πάλαι μὲν Δικαιαρχίαν ὀνομαζομένην, νυνὶ δὲ Ποτιόλους, ἔνιοι τῶν σκευαζόντων τὸ ψιμύθιον ὁμοίως τῷ ῥοδιακῷ λευκὸν εἰώθασι ποιεῖν. ὅτι δὲ ἐν ἡλίῳ τε χρὴ σκευάζειν τὴν ἔμπλαστρον, ὕδατός τε μιγνύειν, ὡς λευκοτέραν τε ἅμα καὶ ψυκτικωτέραν γενέσθαι, λέλεκται πρόσθεν. εἰ δ’ ἐπουλωτικωτέραν ἐθέλοι τις αὐτὴν ἐργάσασθαι, τοῦ μὲν ὕδατος οὐδ’ ὅλως μικτέον ἐστὶ, δι’ ἐλαίου δὲ παλαιοῦ μόνον σκεναστέον γο β΄. ἥμισυ, ἀλλὰ καὶ τρεῖς ἐμβάλλοντα. τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον οὐ τῶν ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ πρώτου συντεθέντων ἐστὶν, ἀλλ’ ἤδη πρὸ πολλῶν ἐτῶν ὑπὸ τοῦ βασιλεύσαντος ἡμῶν τῶν Περγαμηνῶν Ἀττάλου σπουδάσαντος ἀνδρὸς περὶ φάρμακα παντοῖα, καὶ σύγκειται λόγῳ τῷ προειρημένῳ καὶ κρεῖττον αὐτοῦ θαυμάζοιμεν ἂν, εἴ τι δύναται τῶν ὁμοειδῶν συντεθῆναι. μέμνηται δὲ αὐτοῦ καὶ ὁ Ἥρας ἐν τῷ βιβλίῳ τῶν φαρμάκων, ὃ τινὲς μὲν ἐπιγράφουσι νάρθηκα, τινὲς δὲ τόνον δυνάμεων, ἀξιῶν τὸ μὲν ἔλαιον εἶναι παλαιὸν, ὕδατος δ’ αὐτῷ μίγνυσθαι τὸ ἴσον ἢ τὸ ἥμισυ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: γο β΄. ἥμισυ, σκεναστέον, τόνον δυνάμεων

One must put in the white lead called Rhodian, if one wishes the 416 plaster to come out very white. But also in the city formerly named Dicaearchia and now Puteoli, some of those who prepare white lead are accustomed to make it white in the same way as the Rhodian. That one must prepare the plaster in the sun, and mix in water, so that it becomes at once whiter and more cooling, has been stated earlier. But if someone wishes to make it more cicatrizing, no water at all is to be mixed in, but it is to be prepared with old oil alone, putting in two and a half ounces (γο β΄ ἥμισυ), or even three. This drug is not one of those first composed by me, but already many years ago by Attalus, who was king of us Pergamenes, a man who devoted himself to drugs of every kind; and it is composed according to the rationale stated above, and we would be astonished if any of the drugs of the same kind could be composed better than it. Heras too mentions it in his book of drugs, which some entitle Narthex, others Tonos of Remedies (τόνον δυνάμεων), judging that the oil should be old, and that water equal to it, or half as much, be mixed with it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σκεναστέον (read as σκευαστέον), γο β΄. ἥμισυ (unit abbreviation unclear; taken as 'two and a half' of the oil measure), τόνον δυνάμεων (book title; rendering tentative), λόγῳ (taken as 'proportion')

One should put in the so-called Rhodian white lead if one wishes the 416 plaster to turn out quite white. But in the city formerly called Dicaearchia and now Puteoli, too, some of those who prepare white lead are accustomed to make it white, like the Rhodian. That one should prepare the plaster in the sun, and mix in water, so that it becomes at once whiter and more cooling, has been said before. If one wishes to make it more cicatrizing, no water at all is to be mixed in; it is to be prepared with old oil only, putting in two and a half [γο β΄ ἥμισυ: unit uncertain, presumably pounds], or even three. This drug is not among those first composed by me. It was composed many years ago by Attalus, who was king of us Pergamenes, a man who took pains over drugs of every kind; it is compounded in the proportion stated above, and we should be surprised if anything of the same kind could be compounded better than it. Heras too mentions it in his book of drugs, which some entitle Narthex and others Tonos dynameōn [the Strength of Powers]; he requires that the oil be old, and that an equal amount of water, or half as much, be mixed with it.

Greek (Kühn)

τὰ δ’ ἄλλα τῆς συμμετρίας, ﻿ 417 ὡς ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ γέγραπται, πλὴν ὅτι κοτύλας ἐκεῖνος, οὐ λίτρας ἔγραψε, τοῦ τε ὕδατος καὶ τοῦ ἐλαίου μὴ δηλώσας ὁπόσων οὐγγιῶν βούλεται εἶναι τὴν κοτύλην, ἤτοι σταθμικῶν ἢ μετρικῶν. αἱ μὲν γὰρ σταθμικαὶ τὸ βάρος κρίνουσι τῶν σωμάτων, αἱ δὲ μετρικαὶ τὸν ὄγκον. θεραπεύειν μὲν οὖν λέγων τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο γερόντων ἕλκη καὶ ἁπαλοχρώτων ἀληθεύει· πλέον δὲ ἢ δεῖ χαρίζεται, πᾶν ἕλκος ἰᾶσθαι φάσκων. ὅσα μὲν γὰρ ὑγρότερα καὶ διὰ τοῦτο δυσεπούλωτα θεραπεύει ταῦτα, τῶν κακοήθων δ’ οὐδὲν, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ τῶν ἐναίμων τι τραυμάτων· ὁ δέ γε καὶ ταῦτα πάντα φησίν. ἀποσύρματα μέντοι λέγων αὐτὸ θεραπεύειν ἀληθεύει, πολλάκις γὰρ οὕτως ἀποσεσυρμένου, ὡς ἀποκρέμασθαι τὸ δέρμα μετὰ τῆς ὑποκειμένης ἐπί τινος σαρκὸς, ἐπιθεὶς τὸ φάρμακον ἐθεασάμην ἑνωθὲν αὐτῇ τὸ ἀποσεσυρμένον, καὶ τό γε τούτου μεῖζον, ἐνίοτε μελαινόμενον ἤδη.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπί τινος, ἑνωθὲν αὐτῇ

As for the rest of the proportions, 417 they are as I have written them, except that he wrote kotylai, not litrai, of the water and of the oil, without indicating of how many ounces he wants the kotyle to be, whether weight-ounces or measure-ounces. For the weight-ounces judge the heaviness of bodies, the measure-ounces their bulk. Now in saying that this drug treats the ulcers of old men and of those with tender skin he speaks the truth; but he grants it more than he ought, when he asserts that it heals every ulcer. For it treats those that are rather moist and for that reason hard to cicatrize, but none of the malignant ones, just as it treats none of the bleeding wounds either; yet he says it treats all these too. However, in saying that it treats abrasions (aposyrmata, cases where the skin has been torn away) he speaks the truth; for often, when the skin had been torn away on someone in such a way that it hung off together with the underlying flesh, I applied the drug and saw the torn-away part united with it, and—what is greater than this—sometimes when it was already turning black.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπί τινος σαρκὸς, ἀποσύρματα

The rest of the proportion 417 is as I have written it, except that he wrote kotylai, not litrai, for both the water and the oil, without making clear of how many ounces he means the kotylē to be, whether ounces by weight or ounces by measure. For those by weight judge the heaviness of bodies, those by measure their bulk. Now when he says that this drug treats the ulcers of old men and of the soft-skinned, he speaks the truth; but he is more generous than he ought to be when he claims that it heals every ulcer. For it treats those that are moister and for this reason hard to cicatrise, but none of the malignant ones, just as it treats none of the bloody wounds either; yet he says it treats all of these too. When, however, he says that it treats abrasions, he speaks the truth. For often, when in some patient the skin had been stripped off so far that it hung down together with the underlying flesh, I applied the drug and saw the stripped-off part united to it again; and, what is greater than this, sometimes when it was already turning black.
